Hilbert schemes of points on fold-like curves and their combinatorics
Ángel David Ríos Ortiz, Javier Sendra Arranz

Abstract
. We investigate the Hilbert scheme of points on curves with n-fold singularities, that is curves that look locally around their singular points as the axis in an affine space. We describe the structure and number of its irreducible components, and provide a detailed analysis of their singularities, revealing rich combinatorial patterns governing its geometry.



Introduction

The Hilbert scheme of
m
points on a variety
X
over
\mathbb{C}
, which in this paper will be denoted by
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X)
, is one of the most studied objects in algebraic geometry because it not only parametrizes how points in
X
behave when they start to collide, but because of its beauty and complexity. Starting from curves, the geometry of Hilbert schemes of points tends to be very rich. In the case of smooth curves, these Hilbert schemes are isomorphic to the symmetric product of the curve, which is smooth. However, when we allow singularities on the curves, their geometry becomes much more complicated, cf. for example
[
17
,
19
]
and references therein. If an integral curve has
locally planar singularities
, meaning that it is contained in a smooth surface, then the Hilbert scheme of points is irreducible
[
2
,
5
]
, and its singularities are in a deep relation with the compactified Jacobian of the curve, see e.g.
[
8
,
21
]
and the survey
[
22
]
for recent applications to knot theory.

When the curve ceases to be locally planar, there has been growing interest in obtaining new
invariants

[
1
,
18
]
that might serve as substitutes for the topological invariants which are effective in the case of plane curve singularities. In this vein, the study of the geometry of the Hilbert scheme of points associated with these more general singularities becomes particularly meaningful.

One of the main features of Hilbert schemes of points is the so called
Murphy’s law

[
28
,
14
]
that essentially says that, in general, it is out of reach to understand them. Therefore, finding explicit descriptions for the Hilbert schemes of points for specific varieties usually yields to a good amount of geometry. Ran in
[
23
]
studied the case of nodal singularities on curves, he gave a very precise description of the Hilbert scheme of points of an irreducible curve with nodal singularities, describing completely their structure. The case of curves which are not contained in smooth surfaces, to the knowledge of the authors, has not been explored yet.

In this work, we address the Hilbert scheme of points for a class of curves with
rational
n
–fold singularities
, for which we found fascinating geometry and combinatorics. Given a reduced curve
C
, a point
p\in C
is a rational
n
–fold singularity of
C
if locally around
p
, the curve
C
is analytically isomorphic to the union of the axis in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Nodal singularities are the case
n=2
and when
n\geq 3
they are no longer locally planar. Rational
n
–fold singularities have been studied because, as nodal singularities, they are
semi-normal

[
4
,
6
]
. In a very recent work
[
11
]
the authors construct an alternative compactification of the moduli space of curves by adding
stable and separating
fold-like curves, cf.
[
11
, Theorem 1.2]
, see also
[
26
]
.

With this motivation, and with the aim of describing explicitly their compactified Jacobians, we need to first study their Hilbert scheme of points. Suppose now that
C
is an irreducible curve whose unique singularity is a rational
n
–fold singularity. One of our main results is a precise characterization of the irreducible components of its Hilbert scheme of points.

Theorem A
.

Let
C
be an irreducible curve with a unique rational
n
–fold singularity and denote by
C_{\mathrm{sm}}
its smooth locus. The irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
are birational to

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C_{\mathrm{sm}})&\text{ and }&\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m-m^{\prime}}(C_{\mathrm{sm}})\times\mathrm{Gr}(n+1-m^{\prime},n)\,\,\,\text{ for }2\leq m^{\prime}\leq\min\{m,n-1\}.\end{array}

In particular, the number of irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
is
\mathrm{min}\{n-1,m\}
.

A direct consequence of
A
is that the number of irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
is
n-1
as long as
m\geq n-1
(see
Fig.

1
).
As far as the authors are aware, this phenomenon is rather unexpected, since it is typically observed that as the number of points increases, a non-irreducible Hilbert scheme of points tends to have an increasing number of components. Notice also that there exists components of different dimensions whenever
n\geq 4
and
m\geq 2
. So in these cases, the Hilbert scheme of points is not Cohen-Macaulay by
[
7
, Corollary 18.11]
.

n-1
n-1
m
nº of irreducible components

Figure 1
.
Graph of the number of irreducible components of the Hilbert scheme of
m
points on a curve with an
n
-fold singularity.

The strategy to prove
A
is to calculate the
elementary components
of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
. To do so, we first study the locus in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
of those subschemes supported at the singularity, the so-called
punctual Hilbert scheme

\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{p}^{m}(C)
. Since this is a local problem we can assume that we are studying the axis in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, which we denote by
X_{n}
, and the singular point to be the origin
\mathbf{0}
. We classify the possible ideals that appear in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
. From there, we obtain the irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
and also the identification with the corresponding Grassmannians.

With this observation in place, we proceed to examine the structure of the Hilbert scheme of points. For this purpose, we make use of the combinatorial structures associated with these curves; in particular, we derive the following result concerning
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
.

Theorem B
.

The punctual Hilbert scheme
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
is a union of Grassmanians of the form
\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}\nolimits(l,n)
for
\max\{1,n+1-m\}\leq l\leq n-1
, where
\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}\nolimits(l,n)
appears
\binom{l+m-2}{n-1}
–times. Moreover,
there is a well-defined moment map

\mu_{m}:\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})\longrightarrow(m-1)\cdot\Delta_{n-1}.

The moment map allows us to study the geometry of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
through the combinatorics of a
hypersimplicial complex
in
(m-1)\cdot\Delta_{n-1}
.
The strategy for the proof of
B
is motivated by the natural toric action on
X_{n}
. We identify which irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
are lifted to
elementary components
of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
and relate them to the combinatorics of the moment map. To this end, we carry out a detailed analysis of the singularities and scheme structure of the local Hilbert scheme, employing deformation theory and the natural torus action to extend our results to the global setting. This leads to our other main theorem, which provides a detailed description of the singularities occurring in its components. Here, the combinatorics developed previously play a prominent role.

Theorem C
.

The Hilbert scheme
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
is reduced. Moreover, each smoothable component of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
is normal and has toric singularities. Each non-smoothable component is smooth.

When we replace
X_{n}
by an
irreducible
curve
C
with a unique rational
n
–fold singularity, the smoothable component is no longer normal and its singularities are not toric. Moreover, the non-smoothable components are singular. However, their structure can be made explicit. This yields our final main result.

Theorem D
.

Let
C
be an irreducible curve with a unique rational
n
–fold singularity. Then,
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
is reduced. The singularities of the smoothable components are locally unions of normal toric varieties, while those of the non-smoothable components are locally unions of affine spaces.
In addition, there is an explicit description of the normalization of the non-smoothable components.

Structure of the paper

In
Section

1
the main result is
1.13
, where we classify the irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
. In
Section

2
we construct a moment map for
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
leading to
2.1
, obtaining
B
for the reduced structure of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
. In this section, we also explore the relation between the geometry of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
and a hypersimplicial complex. Some of the combinatoric lemmas needed for this purpose are given in
Appendix

A
. In
Section

3
, we prove
3.6
that establishes
A
for the reduced structure of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
. In
Section

4
we deduce
4.11
that shows that the punctual Hilbert scheme is reduced. This completes the proof of
B
. Then by
Section

3
we obtain that the same happens for the whole Hilbert scheme of points, completing the proof of
A
. This makes use of some amount of commutative algebra computations which are given in
Appendix

B
. From there, in
Section

5
we start studying the singular locus of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
where, by using combinatoric methods explained in
Appendix

A
, we characterize it completely. The main result of this section is
5.2
.
Afterwards in
Section

6
we focus on the description of the smoothable and non-smoothable components. Propositions
6.5
and
6.9
complete the proof of Theorem
C
. Theorem
6.3
and Corollaries
6.6
and
6.10
lead to Theorem
D
.
Finally in
Section

7
we report some ongoing work and state some open questions and future research directions.

The theory presented in this paper is complemented by a variety of examples, intended to offer deeper insight into the problems under consideration and to illustrate the geometric structures that emerge from them.

Notations

We will work over
\mathbb{C}
. All arguments remain valid over any algebraically closed field of characteristic zero. The case of positive characteristic is unknown to the authors. Vectors in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
are written in boldface. The origin in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
is denoted by
\mathbf{0}
and the vector
(1,\dots,1)
as
\mathbf{1}
. The standard basis for
\mathbb{C}^{n}
is
\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{n}
. For a subset
S\subset[n]
, we set

\mathbf{e}_{S}:=\sum_{i\in S}\mathbf{e}_{i}.

As is customary,
\binom{[n]}{l}
denotes the set of subsets of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
with
l
elements.

•

X_{n}
: The union of the axis
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}
, where
L_{i}:=\mathbb{V}(x_{j}:j\neq i)\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n}
(
1.1
).

•

R_{n}
: The coordinate ring of
X_{n}
(
Eq.

1
).

•

\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
: The
m
–th symmetric product of
C
(
3.5
).

•

\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
: The reduced Hilbert scheme of
m
points of
X_{n}
(
1.2
).

•

\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
: The punctual Hilbert scheme at the origin (
1.2
).

•

\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
: The reduced punctual Hilbert scheme at the origin (
1.2
).

•

\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
: A subvariety of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
constructed by an integer
l\leq n
and a partition
\mathbf{u}
(
1.5
).

•

\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}}
: The vector subspace generated by monomials indexed by
\mathbf{u}
(
1.5
).

•

\langle\Gamma\rangle
: The ideal generated by the vector subspace
\Gamma\subseteq\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}}
(
Eq.

2
).

•

\kappa(\mathbf{w},k)
: The function that gives the indexes
i
for which
\mathbf{w}_{i}=k
(
Eq.

6
).

•

\mu_{l,n}
: The moment map (
Eq.

10
).

•

\mu_{\mathbf{u},l}
: The moment map defined on
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
(
Eq.

11
).

•

\mu_{m}
: The moment map defined on
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
(
2.1
).

•

\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
: The
(n,m)
–hypersimplicial complex (
A.2
).

•

\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}(S_{1},S_{2},l,\mathbf{u})
: The faces of the complex
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
(
Eq.

14
).

•

\mathcal{K}_{l,n}^{[m]}
: subcomplex of
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
formed by hypersimplices of the form
\Delta_{l,n}
.

•

\mathcal{G}_{n}^{m}
: The variety obtained by gluing Grassmannians following
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
(
Eq.

16
).

•

\mathcal{G}_{l,n}^{m}
: The subvariety of
\mathcal{G}_{n}^{m}
formed by the Grassmannians of the form
\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}\nolimits(l,n)
.

•

\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m,m^{\prime}}(X_{n})
: The non-smoothable components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
(
Eq.

23
).

•

\mathbb{S}_{k}
and
\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{k}
: The simplicial complexes describing the singularities of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
(
5.8
).

•

\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m,m^{\prime},u}(C)
: The strata of the non-smoothable components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
(
Eq.

45
).

1.
Irreducible components of the punctual Hilbert scheme

A classical strategy to analyze the irreducible components of Hilbert schemes of points is to focus on elementary components. Following
[
12
]
, an elementary component is an irreducible component of the Hilbert scheme of
m
points that parameterizes subschemes supported at a single point. In the case of a curve
C
with a rational
n
–fold singularity
p\in C
, an elementary component must parametrize length
m
subschemes supported at the singularity of
p
. We start this section defining this type of singularities.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
X_{n}
be the union of the axis
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}
of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, where
L_{i}:=\mathbb{V}(x_{j}:j\neq i)
and let
\mathbf{0}=(0,\ldots,0)\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
be the singular point of
X_{n}
. A curve
C
has a rational
n
–fold singularity at
p\in C
if, locally around
p
,
C
is analytically isomorphic to
X_{n}
around
\mathbf{0}
.

Algebraically,
p\in C
is a rational
n
–fold singularity if there exists an isomorphism between the completed stalks

\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{C,p}\simeq\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},\mathbf{0}}.

We denote the coordinate ring of
X_{n}
by
R_{n}
, which is defined by

(1)

R_{n}=\mathbb{C}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]/\langle x_{i}x_{j}:1\leq i<j\leq n\rangle.

A rational
2
–fold singularity is a nodal singularity. However, for
n\geq 3
such singularities have embedding dimension
n
; in particular, they can no longer be embedded in a smooth surface.
By the very definition of elementary components, we can replace
C
and
p
by
X_{n}
and
\mathbf{0}
, respectively. In this section, we analyze ideals in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
supported at
\mathbf{0}
, and then extend the analysis to the full Hilbert scheme. We first perform this analysis on the reduced Hilbert scheme of points
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
, which is
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
endowed with its reduced structure.

Definition 1.2
.

The punctual Hilbert scheme at
\mathbf{0}
, denoted by
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
, is the locus in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
of length
m
ideals in
R_{n}
supported at
\mathbf{0}
. The variety
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
is the punctual Hilbert scheme endowed with its
reduced structure
.

Notice that an elementary component of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
is an irreducible component of

\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
. We first study the irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
, which can be done within
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
. In
Section

3
, we determine which of these lift to elementary components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
.

Figure 2
.
Planes spanned by each pairs of lines in
X_{3}
.

Example 1.3
.

For
m=2
,
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{2}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})=\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{2}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
is the projectivization of the tangent space of
X_{n}
at
\mathbf{0}
. In particular,
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{2}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
is
\mathbb{P}(T_{\mathbf{0}}X_{n})\simeq\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
and we can identify ideals in
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{2}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
with the tangent directions at
\mathbf{0}
. The intersection of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{2}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
and the smoothable component consists of all tangent directions at
\mathbf{0}
that lie in the planes spanned by two of the lines
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}
. This is also called the
tangent star
of
X_{n}
(see
Fig.

2
). We refer to
[
24
, Chapter 1]
for the general definition of the tangent star.
In particular,
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{2}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})\simeq\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
must be an irreducible component of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{2}(X_{n})
. This fact can also be derived by a dimension argument.

We now give a description of the generators of the ideals in
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
.

Proposition 1.4
.

Let
l\in[n]
and
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{n}
be a strictly positive partition of
m+l-1
. Consider a full rank matrix
A
of size
l\times n
with no vanishing column. Then, the ideal generated by the polynomials

\begin{pmatrix}f_{1}\\
\vdots\\
f_{l}\end{pmatrix}=A\,\,\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}^{u_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
x_{n}^{u_{n}}\end{pmatrix}.

lies in
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
. Moreover, all ideals in
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
are of this form.

Proof.

Let
J
be an ideal as above. First we check that
J
is supported at
\mathbf{0}
. Since
A
has not vanishing columns, for every
1\leq i\leq n
there exists
1\leq j\leq l
such that the entry
A_{j,i}
of
A
is nonzero. Therefore it holds
x_{i}f_{j}=A_{j,i}x_{i}^{u_{i}+1}
. We deduce that
J
contains
x_{1}^{u_{1}+1},\ldots,x_{n}^{u_{n}+1}
and therefore,
J
is supported at
\mathbf{0}
. We are left to show that
J
has length
m
. Since
x_{1}^{u_{1}+1},\ldots,x_{n}^{u_{n}+1}\in J
, the quotient
R_{n}/J
is generated by
1,x_{1},\ldots,x_{1}^{u_{1}},\ldots,x_{n},\ldots,x_{n}^{u_{n}}
. The generators of
J
induce
l
linearly independent linear relations among
x_{1}^{u_{1}},\ldots,x_{n}^{u_{n}}
. We deduce that

\mathop{\mathrm{dim}}\nolimits R_{n}/J=1+u_{1}+\cdots+u_{n}-l=|\mathbf{u}|+1-l=m

as claimed.

We will now prove the second part of the proposition. Let
J
be an ideal of
R_{n}
supported at
\mathbf{0}
of length
m
, and let
f_{1},\ldots,f_{l}
be minimal generators of
J
. Since
J
is supported at
\mathbf{0}
, then
f_{1},\ldots,f_{l}
do not have independent term. We write each
f_{i}
as

f_{i}=f_{i,1}(x_{1})+\cdots+f_{i,n}(x_{n}),

where
f_{i,j}(x_{i})
is a polynomial in
x_{i}
such that
f_{i,j}(0)=0
. In the case of
x_{1}
we get

f_{i,1}=\sum_{k=1}^{d_{i}}a_{i,j}x_{1}^{j},

where
d_{i}=\mathop{\mathrm{deg}}\nolimits f_{i,1}
and
a_{i,j}\in\mathbb{C}
. Notice that if
f_{i,1}
vanishes for all
i
, then the dimension of
R_{n}/J
as a
\mathbb{C}
–vector space is infinite, and this implies that
J
does not have finite length. Therefore we can assume that
a_{1,d_{1}}
is nonzero. Now, in
R_{n}
we have that

x_{1}f_{1}=x_{1}f_{i,1}=\sum_{j=1}^{d_{1}}a_{1,j}x_{1}^{j+1}

is in
J
. Since
J
is supported at
\mathbf{0}
, the only root of
x_{1}f_{i,1}
is
0
. We conclude that
f_{i,1}
is the monomial
a_{i,d_{i}}x_{1}^{d_{i}+1}
. We can assume
d_{1}
is the minimum between those
d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}
that are nonzero. By replacing
f_{i}
by
a_{1,d_{1}}f_{i}-a_{i,d_{i}}x_{1}^{d_{i}-d_{1}}f_{1}
, we may assume that
d_{1}=d_{2}=\cdots=d_{n}
.

Let
d_{i}
be the degree of
f_{i,1}
and suppose that there exists
2\leq i\leq l
such that
d_{i}>d_{1}
. In
R_{n}
we have that the monomial

x_{1}^{d_{i}-d_{1}}f_{1}=x_{1}^{d_{i}-d_{1}}f_{1,1}=\sum_{j=1}^{d_{1}}a_{1,j}x_{1}^{j+d_{i}-d_{1}}

is in
J
. Since
J
is supported at
\mathbf{0}
, the only root of
x_{1}^{d_{i}-d_{1}}f_{1,1}
is
0
. We conclude that
f_{i,1}
is the monomial
a_{1,d_{1}}x_{1}^{d_{1}}
. Repeating this process with the variables
x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}
we get that there exist
d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}
such that
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
are a linear combination of
x_{1}^{d_{1}},\ldots,x_{n}^{d_{n}}
.

∎

Notice that in the extremal case
l=n
in
1.4
leads to the ideal
\langle x_{1}^{u_{1}},\ldots,x_{n}^{u_{n}}\rangle
.

Definition 1.5
.

Given
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{n}
, let
\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}}:=\langle x_{1}^{u_{1}},\ldots,x_{n}^{u_{n}}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
be the
\mathbb{C}
–vector space generated by
x_{1}^{u_{1}},\ldots,x_{n}^{u_{n}}
.
Fix
m\geq 1
. For
l\in[n]
such that
|\mathbf{u}|=m+l-1
, the subvariety
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})\subseteq\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
is the closure of the ideals of the form
\langle f_{1},\ldots,f_{l}\rangle
where
f_{1},\ldots,f_{l}
are linearly independent elements of
\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}}
.

With the notation introduced above we immediately obtain the following.

Corollary 1.6
.

Let
m,n
be positive integers, then there is a decomposition

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})=\bigcup_{l=1}^{n}\bigcup_{{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{n}\\
|\mathbf{u}|=m+l-1\end{array}}}\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u}).

Hence the varieties
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
are the candidates to be irreducible components of the punctual Hilbert scheme. Next, we will describe the geometry of these varieties. For any
\Gamma\in\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}\nolimits(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}})
, let
\langle\Gamma\rangle
be the ideal generated by
\Gamma
in
R_{n}
. Define the rational map

(2)

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\varphi_{l,\mathbf{u}}:&\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}\nolimits(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}})&\dashrightarrow&\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})\\
&\Gamma&\mapsto&\langle\Gamma\rangle.\end{array}

Since
m+l-1=|\mathbf{u}|
, by
1.4
the map
\varphi_{l,\mathbf{u}}
is well–defined in an open subset of the Grassmannian.

Lemma 1.7
.

The base locus of
\varphi_{l,\mathbf{u}}
is contained in the union

(3)

\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{H}_{i},

where
\mathcal{H}_{i}:=\{\Gamma\in\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}\nolimits(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}}):\Gamma\subseteq\langle x_{j}^{u_{j}}:j\neq i\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}\}
.

Proof.

If
\Gamma\in\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}\nolimits(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}})
, then
\langle\Gamma\rangle
is an ideal of
R_{n}
supported at
\mathbf{0}
. Hence, the base locus of
\varphi_{l,\mathbf{u}}
coincide with the locus of
\Gamma\in\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}\nolimits(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}})
such that
\langle\Gamma\rangle
is not a length
m
ideal. Now, if
\Gamma\in\mathcal{H}_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
, then
\langle\Gamma\rangle
has no finite length. Assume now that
\Gamma\not\in\mathcal{H}_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
. Then,
\langle\Gamma\rangle
is generated by
l
polynomials
f_{1},\ldots,f_{l}
of the form

f_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i,j}x_{j}^{u_{i}}.

Since
\Gamma\not\in\mathcal{H}_{i}
for all
i
, we deduce that for any
1\leq i\leq n
, there exists
1\leq j\leq l
such that
a_{i,j}\neq 0
. In particular,
x_{1}^{u_{1}+1},\ldots,x_{n}^{u_{n}+1}
are contained in
\langle\Gamma\rangle
, and hence,
\langle\Gamma\rangle
has finite length. Moreover, this length is given by
u_{1}+\cdots+u_{n}+1-l=|\mathbf{u}|+1-l=m
. We conclude that
\varphi_{l,\mathbf{u}}
is well–defined away from (
3
).
∎

In the next lemma we will show that the map
\varphi_{l,\mathbf{u}}
can be extended along (
3
).

Lemma 1.8
.

With the notation of
1.7
, let
1\leq k\leq n
and assume
\Gamma\in\mathcal{H}_{i_{1}}\cap\cdots\cap\mathcal{H}_{i_{k}}
where
1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq n
and
\Gamma\not\in\mathcal{H}_{j}
for
j\neq i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}
. Then

\varphi_{l,\mathbf{u}}(\Gamma):=\langle\Gamma\rangle+\langle x_{i_{1}}^{u_{i_{1}}+1},\ldots,x_{i_{k}}^{u_{i_{k}}+1}\rangle

extends
\varphi_{l,\mathbf{u}}
to all
\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}\nolimits(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}})
.

Proof.

Let
J
be an ideal in the image of
\varphi_{l,\mathbf{u}}
. By construction,
x_{1}^{u_{1}+1},\ldots,x_{n}^{u_{n}+1}
are contained in
J
. In other words,
\langle x_{1}^{u_{1}+1},\ldots,x_{n}^{u_{n}+1}\rangle
is contained in
J
. Since this containment is a closed condition in
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
, we deduce that for any
J
in the closure of the image of
\varphi_{l,\mathbf{u}}
, we have that
\langle x_{1}^{u_{1}+1},\ldots,x_{n}^{u_{n}+1}\rangle\subseteq J
.

Let
\Gamma
be an element of the base locus of
\varphi_{l,\mathbf{u}}
. By
1.7
, there exist
1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq n
such that
\Gamma\in\mathcal{H}_{i_{1}}\cap\cdots\cap\mathcal{H}_{i_{k}}
for
1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq n
such that
\Gamma\not\in\mathcal{H}_{j}
for
j\neq i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}
. In particular,
x_{j}^{u_{j}+1}
is contained in
\langle\Gamma\rangle
for
j\neq i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}
.
Now, let
C
be a smooth curve in
\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}\nolimits(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}})
passing through
\Gamma
and not contained in the base locus of
\varphi_{l,\mathbf{u}}
. Then, the restriction of
\varphi_{l,\mathbf{u}}
to
C
extents to all
C
. Let
I
be the image of
\Gamma
via this extension. Note that by construction,
\langle\Gamma\rangle
is contained in
I
. Therefore, we deduce that

(4)

\langle\Gamma\rangle+\langle x_{i_{1}}^{u_{i_{1}}+1},\ldots,x_{i_{k}}^{u_{i_{k}}+1}\rangle\subseteq I.

Both ideals in (
4
) are the same since both have length
m
. Then the proof follows from the fact that the ideal
I
does not depend on the curve
C
.
∎

Remark 1.9
.

The varieties
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
in
1.5
are given as the closure of the ideals minimally generated by
f_{1},\ldots,f_{l}\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}}
. Using
1.8
, there is a complete description of the elements in the boundary of this closure. This boundary is exactly the image of (
3
) through
\varphi_{l,\mathbf{u}}
.

Proposition 1.10
.

The map
\varphi_{l,\mathbf{u}}
extends uniquely to an isomorphism
\mathrm{Gr}(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}})\cong\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
.

Proof.

The inverse of
\varphi_{l,\mathbf{u}}
is the map

\psi_{l,\mathbf{u}}\colon\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})\dashrightarrow\mathrm{Gr}(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}})

that associates to an ideal
J
in
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
the linear subspace in
\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}}
generated by its minimal set of generators, whenever this has dimension
l
. The only case where this does not happen is in the boundary of
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
. By
1.9
, such an ideal is, up to labeling, of the form

(5)

\langle f_{1},\ldots,f_{l}\rangle+\langle x_{k}^{u_{k}+1},\ldots,x_{n}^{u_{n}+1}\rangle,

where
f_{1},\ldots,f_{l}\in\langle x_{1}^{u_{1}},\ldots,x_{k-1}^{u_{k-1}}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
are linearly independent and
1\leq k\leq n
. Then, the extension of
\psi_{l,\mathbf{u}}
sends
J
to
\langle f_{1},\ldots,f_{l}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
.
∎

Using the map
\varphi_{l,\mathbf{u}}
, we can understand the intersection of two varieties of the form
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
and
\Sigma(m,l^{\prime},\mathbf{v})
. Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Define

(6)

\kappa(\mathbf{w},k):=\{i\in[n]:\mathbf{w}_{i}=k\}\subseteq[n].

Proposition 1.11
.

Let
l,l^{\prime}\in[n-1]
and
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{n}
such that
|\mathbf{u}|=m+l-1
and
|\mathbf{v}|=m+l^{\prime}-1
. Then,
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})\cap\Sigma(m,l^{\prime},\mathbf{v})
is nonempty if and only if
\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1,-1\}^{n}\setminus\{\mathbf{1},-\mathbf{1}\}
. In this case, the intersection
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})\cap\Sigma(m,l^{\prime},\mathbf{v})
consists on the ideals of the form:

(7)

\langle f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\rangle+\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}}:i\in\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},1)\rangle+\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}+1}:i\in\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},-1)\rangle,

with
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\in\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}}:i\in\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},0)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
linearly independent and
r=l-|\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},1)|
.

Proof.

Let
[I]\in\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
. Then by (
5
) there exist
S\subseteq[n]
such that

I=\langle f_{1},\ldots,f_{l}\rangle+\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}+1}:i\in S\rangle,

with
f_{1},\ldots,f_{l}\in\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}}:i\not\in S\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
linearly independent. With this presentation some of the generators
f_{1},\ldots,f_{l}
might be equal to
x_{i}^{u_{i}}
for
i\not\in S
. Rewrite
I
as

(8)

I=\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}+1}:i\in S\rangle+\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}}:i\in T\rangle+\langle f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\rangle,

where
T\subset[n]
is such that
S\cap T=\emptyset
,
r=l-|T|
and
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\in\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}}:i\not\in S\cup T\rangle.

Analogously, if
[I]\in\Sigma(m,l^{\prime},\mathbf{v})
, then there exists
S^{\prime},T^{\prime}\subsetneq[n]
disjoint such that

(9)

I=\langle x_{i}^{v_{i}+1}:i\in S^{\prime}\rangle+\langle x_{i}^{v_{i}}:i\in T^{\prime}\rangle+\langle f_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,f_{r^{\prime}}^{\prime}\rangle,

with
r^{\prime}=l^{\prime}-|T^{\prime}|
and
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r^{\prime}}\in\langle x_{i}^{v_{i}}:i\not\in S^{\prime}\cup T^{\prime}\rangle.
From (
8
) and (
9
) we deduce that
r=r^{\prime}
,
\langle f_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,f_{r^{\prime}}^{\prime}\rangle=\langle f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\rangle
and
S\cup T=S^{\prime}\cup T^{\prime}
. Therefore,
u_{i}=v_{i}
for
i\not\in S\cup T
. For
i\in S
we get that
u_{i}+1=v_{i}
or
u_{i}+1=v_{i}+1
. Similarly,
u_{i}=v_{i}
or
u_{i}=v_{i}+1
for
i\in T
. Hence,
u_{i}-v_{i}\in\{0,1,-1\}
for any
i\in[n]
. Finally, notice that
S\neq[n]
, since the ideal
\langle x_{1}^{u_{1}+1},\ldots,x_{n}^{u_{n}+1}\rangle
has length
|\mathbf{u}|+n+1-n=m+l
which is greater than
m
and the same holds for
S^{\prime}
. This shows the first inclusion.

For the other inclusion, assume that
\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1,-1\}^{n}\setminus\{\mathbf{1},-\mathbf{1}\}
and let
J
be an ideal as in (
7
). Then
[J]\in\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
since it is the image of

\langle f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}+\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}}:i\in\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},1)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}}

via
\varphi_{l,\mathbf{u}}
. Similarly, we can rewrite
J
as

\langle f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\rangle+\langle x_{i}^{v_{i}+1}:i\in\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},1)\rangle+\langle x_{i}^{v_{i}}:i\in\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},-1)\rangle,

which is the image of

\langle f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}+\langle x_{i}^{v_{i}}:i\in\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},-1)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq\Lambda_{\mathbf{v}}

via
\varphi_{\mathbf{v},l^{\prime}}
. We conclude that
[J]\in\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})\cap\Sigma(m,l^{\prime},\mathbf{v})
.
∎

Corollary 1.12
.

Let
l,l^{\prime}\in[n-1]
and
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{n}
such that
|\mathbf{u}|=m+l-1
and
|\mathbf{v}|=m+l^{\prime}-1
and
\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1,-1\}^{n}\setminus\{\mathbf{1},-\mathbf{1}\}
. Then

\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})\cap\Sigma(m,l^{\prime},\mathbf{v})\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}\nolimits(l-|\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},1)|,|\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},0)|),

where the intersection is taken with the reduced structure.

Proof.

Let
U=\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}}:i\in|\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},0)|\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
. We get a closed embedding of
\mathrm{Gr}(l-|\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},1)|,U)
into
\mathrm{Gr}(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}})
by sending
\Gamma
to
\Gamma+\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}}:i\in\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},1)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
. The composition of this closed embedding with
\varphi_{\mathbf{u},l}
gives the isomorphism.
∎

Having described the varieties
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
, we can finally identify which of these varieties are the irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
.

Theorem 1.13
.

The irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
are such
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})

for which
\max\{1,n+1-m\}\leq l\leq n-1
and
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{n}
with
|\mathbf{u}|=m+l-1
.

Proof.

1.6
decomposes
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
as the union of closed subvarieties, each of them irreducible, hence it suffices to show which
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
are irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
.

We will first show that
\Sigma(m,n,\mathbf{u})
is not an irreducible component. If
[J]\in\Sigma(m,n,\mathbf{u})
is generic, then it is minimally generated by
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
where
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
are linearly independent elements of
\langle x_{1}^{u_{1}},\ldots,x_{n}^{u_{n}}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
. Since the latter is an
n
–dimensional vector space we deduce that
J=\langle x_{1}^{u_{1}},\ldots,x_{n}^{u_{n}}\rangle
. Since
|\mathbf{u}|=m+n-1
and
m\geq 2
, there exists
1\leq i\leq n
such that
u_{i}\geq 2
. We claim that
[J]\in\Sigma(m,n-1,\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{e}_{i})
. Indeed, the family of length
m
ideals

J_{\lambda}:=\langle x_{k}^{u_{k}}+\lambda x_{i}^{u_{i}-1}:k\in[n]\setminus\{i\}\rangle

with
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
satisfies
[J_{\lambda}]\in\Sigma(m,n-1,\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{e}_{i})
and for
\lambda=0
this family extends uniquely to
J_{0}=J
. We get that
\Sigma(m,n,\mathbf{u})
is contained in
\Sigma(m,n-1,\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{e}_{i})
and hence, it is not an irreducible component of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
.

Now assume that
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
is not an irreducible component of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
for
1\leq l\leq n-1
. Then, there exists
\mathbf{v}
and
l^{\prime}
such that
\Sigma(m,l^{\prime},\mathbf{v})
is an irreducible component and
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
is contained in
\Sigma(m,l^{\prime},\mathbf{v})
. By
1.11
,
\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1,-1\}^{n}\setminus\{\mathbf{1},-\mathbf{1}\}
. Moreover, by
1.10
and
1.12
we get that

\mathrm{Gr}(l,n)\simeq\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})=\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})\cap\Sigma(m,l^{\prime},\mathbf{v})\simeq\mathrm{Gr}(l-|\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},1)|,|\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},0)|).

We deduce that
|\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},0)|=n
. Hence,
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{v}
and
l=l^{\prime}
which is a contradiction. Therefore,
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
is an irreducible component of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
.
∎

Corollary 1.14
.

The number of irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
is

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\sum_{l=n-m+1}^{n-1}\binom{l+m-2}{n-1}=\frac{(m-1)}{n}\binom{m+n-2}{n-1}&\text{if $n\geq m$},\\
\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{n-1}\binom{m+l-2}{n-1}=\frac{(m-1)}{n}\binom{m+n-2}{n-1}+\frac{(n-m)}{n}\binom{m+n-2}{-1+n}&\text{if $n\leq m$.}\end{cases}

Proof.

Since the number of irreducible components is topological we may work in
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
. By
1.13
, the quantity on the left-hand side is determined, while the expression on the right-hand side follows from classical combinatorial identities.
∎

From the above reasoning we deduce that
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
is the union of some Grassmannians glued together through closed subvarieties.
1.15
shows how this gluing is done for
n=2
.

Example 1.15
.

For
n=2
, the only possible value of
l
is
1
, and
\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},u_{2})
is a strictly positive partition of
m
. Hence

\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{2})=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m-1}\Sigma(m,1,(i,m-i))

has
m-1
irreducible components.
A generic point in
\Sigma(m,1,(i,m-i))
corresponds to an ideal of the form
\langle\lambda_{1}x_{1}^{i}+\lambda_{2}x_{2}^{m-i}\rangle
for
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\neq 0
. One can check that
\Sigma(m,1,(i,m-i))
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, whose torus is identified with the ideals of the above form, and the torus invariant points are the ideals
\langle x_{1}^{i+1},x_{2}^{m-i}\rangle
and
\langle x_{1}^{i},x_{2}^{m-i+1}\rangle
. Moreover, the ideal
\langle x_{1}^{i+1},x_{2}^{m-i}\rangle
is the intersection of
\Sigma(m,1,(i,m-i))
and
\Sigma(m,1,(i+1,m-i_{1}))
. With further work, it can be shown that
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{2})
is a chain of rational curves with nodal singularities obtained by gluing consecutively
\Sigma(m,1,(i,m-i))
and
\Sigma(m,1,(i+1,m-i_{1}))
through the point associated to the ideal
\langle x_{1}^{i+1},x_{2}^{m-i}\rangle
(see
Fig.

3
). This is precisely
[
23
, Theorem 1]
.

\Sigma(m,1,(1,m-1))
\Sigma(m,1,(2,m-2))
\Sigma(m,1,(m-1,1))

Figure 3
.
The
m
rational irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{2})
.

2.
The moment map and its combinatorics

We keep the same notation as in
Section

1
. Our next aim is to describe how
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
is obtained by gluing the Grassmannians
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
using the combinatorial framework developed in
Appendix

A
. To encode the combinatorics of these Grassmannians, we make use of the moment map associated with the natural action of the algebraic torus on
X_{n}
, cf. e.g.
[
3
,
16
]
for more details about moment maps in symplectic and algebraic geometry. We consider the Plücker coordinates
q_{A}
for
A
in
\binom{[n]}{l}
. For
l<n
the moment map
\mu_{l,n}
is:

(10)

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\mu_{l,n}:&\mathrm{Gr}(l,n)&\longrightarrow&\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
&(q_{A})_{A\in\binom{[n]}{l}}&\longmapsto&\displaystyle\frac{1}{\displaystyle\sum_{A}|q_{A}|^{2}}\left(\sum_{A}|q_{A}|^{2}\mathbf{e}_{A}\right).\end{array}

Note that the moment map is not algebraic and it is defined over the
\mathbb{C}
–points of
\mathrm{Gr}(l,n)
.
The image of the moment map is the hypersimplex
\Delta_{l,n}
, which
lies in
l\cdot\Delta_{n-1}
. Here,
l\cdot\Delta_{n-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the dilation by
l
of the
(n-1)
–dimensional simplex. The definition of
\Delta_{l,n}
and the description of its faces can be found in
Appendix

A
.
The vertices of
\Delta_{l,n}
are exactly the vectors
\mathbf{e}_{i_{1}}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{i_{l}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{l}\leq n
, which correspond via
\mu_{l,n}
to the point
[\langle\mathbf{e}_{i_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{e}_{i_{l}}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}]
in
\mathrm{Gr}(l,n)
. More generally, a face

\Delta_{l,n}(S_{1},S_{2})
for
S_{1}\sqcup S_{2}\subseteq[n]
(see
Eq.

49
)
is isomorphic to the hypersimplex
\Delta_{l-|S_{2}|,n-r+1}
, and via the moment map, it corresponds to the variety

\left\{[E]\in\mathrm{Gr}(l,n):\langle\mathbf{e}_{i}:i\in S_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq E\subseteq\langle\mathbf{e}_{i}:i\not\in S_{1}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}\right\},

which is isomorphic to
\mathrm{Gr}(l-|S_{2}|,n-r+1)
.
The goal of this section is to provide a complete combinatorial description of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
by constructing a moment map on it. To do so, we first construct a moment map on each irreducible component. On
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})\simeq\mathrm{Gr}(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}})\simeq\mathrm{Gr}(n-l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}}^{*})
, define the following moment map:

(11)

\mu_{\mathbf{u},l}:\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})\simeq\mathrm{Gr}(n-l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}}^{*})\longrightarrow\Delta_{n-l,n}+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}

sending a point
\Gamma
to
\mu_{n-l,n}(\Gamma^{*})+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}
. In other words, the map
\mu_{\mathbf{u},l}
is the composition of the isomorphism
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})\simeq\mathrm{Gr}(n-l,n)
with the moment map
\mu_{n-l,n}
and the translation by the vector
\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}
. In the Plücker coordinates of
\mathrm{Gr}(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}})
, the moment map
\mu_{\mathbf{u},l}
is given by

(12)

\mu_{\mathbf{u},l}((q_{A})_{A\in\binom{[n]}{l}})=\displaystyle\frac{1}{\displaystyle\sum_{I}|q_{A}|^{2}}\left(\sum_{A}|q_{A}|^{2}\mathbf{e}_{[n]\setminus I}\right)+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}.

Theorem 2.1
.

For distinct pairs
(\mathbf{u},l)
and
(\mathbf{v},l^{\prime})
, the moment maps
\mu_{\mathbf{u},l}
and
\mu_{\mathbf{v},l^{\prime}}
coincide in the intersection of
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
and
\Sigma(m,l^{\prime},\mathbf{v})
. Therefore, there is a well-defined moment map

(13)

\mu_{m}:\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})\longrightarrow(m-1)\Delta_{n-1}

whose restriction to each irreducible component
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
is
\mu_{\mathbf{u},l}
.

Proof.

Let
[I]\in\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})\cap\Sigma(m,l^{\prime},\mathbf{v})
.
By
1.11
,
\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1,-1\}^{n}
and

I=\langle f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\rangle+\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}}:i\in\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},1)\rangle+\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}+1}:i\in\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},-1)\rangle,

with
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\in\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}}:i\in\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},0)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
linearly independent and
r=l-|\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},1)|
. As an element of
\mathrm{Gr}(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}})
,
[I]
corresponds to the linear subspace

\langle f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}+\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}}:i\in\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},1)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}.

We will compute explicitly the moment map in coordinates. Let
M
be the
l\times n
matrix whose rows consists on the coefficients of
x_{1}^{u_{1}},\ldots,x_{n}^{u_{n}}
in
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}
and
x_{i}^{u_{i}}
for
i\in\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},1)
. In other words,
M
is a matrix of the form

M=\begin{pNiceArray},

where
N
is the
r\times|\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},0)|
matrix

N=\begin{pmatrix}\cdots&f_{1}&\cdots\\
&\vdots&\\
\cdots&f_{r}&\cdots\end{pmatrix}

whose rows are the coefficients of
x_{i}^{u_{i}}
for
i\in\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},0)
in
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}
. Thus, the Plücker coordinate
q_{A}
of
[I]
, where
A\in\binom{[n]}{l}
, corresponds to the
l\times A
minor of
M
. We denote this minor by
\det(M,A)
. Note that if
A\cap\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},-1)\neq\emptyset
, the submatrix of
M
given by the columns in
A
has a vanishing column. Thus, we have that
q_{A}=0
if
A\cap\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},-1)\neq\emptyset
. Similarly, if
\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},1)
is not contained in
A
, the submatrix of
M
given by the columns in
A
has a vanishing row. Hence we get
q_{A}=0
if
\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},1)\not\subset A
. Thus, the only nonvanishing Plücker coordinates of
[I]
in
\mathrm{Gr}(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}})
are such
q_{A}
with
A=\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},1)\cup B
for some
B\in\binom{\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},0)}{r}
. In this case,
q_{A}
is the minor
r\times B
of
N
, i.e.
q_{A}=\det(N,B)
. Using (
11
), we get that

\begin{split}\mu_{\mathbf{u},l}([I])&=\displaystyle\frac{1}{\displaystyle\sum_{B\in\binom{\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},0)}{r}}|\det(N,B)|^{2}}\left(\sum_{B\in\binom{\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},0)}{r}}|\det(N,B)|^{2}\mathbf{e}_{[n]\setminus(B\cup\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},1))}\right)+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}\\[-4.0pt]
&=\displaystyle\frac{1}{\displaystyle\sum_{B}|\det(N,B)|^{2}}\left(\sum_{B}|\det(N,B)|^{2}\mathbf{e}_{(\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},0)\setminus B)\cup\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},-1)}\right)+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}\\[-4.0pt]
&=\displaystyle\frac{1}{\displaystyle\sum_{B}|\det(N,B)|^{2}}\left(\sum_{B}|\det(N,B)|^{2}(\mathbf{e}_{\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},0)\cup\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},-1)}-\mathbf{e}_{B})\right)+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}\\[-6.0pt]
&=\mathbf{e}_{\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},0)\cup\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},-1)}-\frac{1}{\displaystyle\sum_{B}|\det(N,B)|^{2}}\left(\sum_{B}|\det(N,B)|^{2}\mathbf{e}_{B})\right)+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}\\[-6.0pt]
&=\mathbf{e}_{\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},0)}+\mathbf{e}_{\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},-1)}-\frac{1}{\displaystyle\sum_{B}|\det(N,B)|^{2}}\left(\sum_{B}|\det(N,B)|^{2}\mathbf{e}_{B})\right)+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}.\end{split}

A similar computation shows that

\mu_{\mathbf{v},l^{\prime}}([I])=\mathbf{e}_{\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},0)}+\mathbf{e}_{\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},1)}-\frac{1}{\displaystyle\sum_{B}|\det(N,B)|^{2}}\left(\sum_{B}|\det(N,B)|^{2}\mathbf{e}_{B})\right)+\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{1}.

The proof follows from the fact that
\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{e}_{\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},1)}-\mathbf{e}_{\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},-1)}
.
∎

Example 2.2
.

Continuing
1.15
, for
n=2
, the
m-1
irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{2})
are
\Sigma(m,1,(1,m-1)),\ldots,\Sigma(m,1,(m-1))
. Each of them is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. The moment map
\mu_{(i,m-i),1}
is defined as

\begin{array}[]{cccccl}\mu_{(i,m-i),1}:&\Sigma(m,1,(i,m-i))\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}&\longrightarrow&(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{*}&\longrightarrow&(m-1)\cdot\Delta_{1}\\
&[a_{0},a_{1}]&\longmapsto&[a_{1},a_{0}]&\longmapsto&(\frac{|a_{1}|^{2}}{|a_{0}|^{2}+|a_{1}|^{2}}+i-1,\frac{|a_{0}|^{2}}{|a_{0}|^{2}+|a_{1}|^{2}}+m-i-1)\end{array}.

Using the above formula a direct computation yields

\begin{array}[]{c}\mu_{(i,m-i),1}(\langle x_{1}^{i+1},x_{2}^{m-i}\rangle)=\mu_{(i,m-i),1}([0,1])=(i,m-i-1)=\mu_{(i+1,m-i-1),1}([1,0])\\
=\mu_{(i+1,m-i-1),1}(\langle x_{1}^{i+1},x_{2}^{m-i}\rangle).\end{array}

In particular,
\mu_{(i,m-i),1}
and
\mu_{(i+1,m-i-1),1}
coincide in the intersection of
\Sigma(m,1,(i,m-i))
and
\Sigma(m,1,(i+1,m-i))
. The image of
\mu_{(i,m-i),1}
is the segment between
(i,m-i-1)
and
(i-1,m-i)
. These segments form a subdivision of
(m-1)\cdot\Delta_{1}
, which is the image of the moment map
\mu_{m}
. In
Fig.

4
, the image of
\mu_{2}
,
\mu_{3}
and
\mu_{4}
is depicted.

{\color[rgb]{0.34,0.65,0}\mu}{{}_{3}}
{\color[rgb]{0.42,0.65,0.95}\mu}{{}_{4}}
+e_{2}
+e_{1}

.
Decomposition of
(m-1)\cdot\Delta_{1}
according to the moment map
\mu_{m}
for
m=2,3,4
and
n=2
. It corresponds to the hypersimplicial complexes
\mathcal{K}^{[2]}_{2}
,
\mathcal{K}^{[3]}_{2}
, and
\mathcal{K}^{[4]}_{2}
.

The image of the moment map
\mu
is the
union of all hypersimplices
\Delta_{n-l,n}+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}
for
\max\{1,n+1-m\}\leq l\leq n-1
and
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{n}
with
\mathbf{u}=m+l-1
.

Definition 2.3
.

The hypersimplices
\Delta_{n-l,n}+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}
for
\max\{1,n+1-m\}\leq l\leq n-1
and
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{n}
with
\mathbf{u}=m+l-1
form a hypersimplicial complex called the
(n,m)
–hypersimplicial complex
. We denote this hypersimplicial complex by
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
.

By
A.2
, the
(n,m)
–hypersimplicial complex
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
is indeed a hypersimplicial complex, and it subdivides
(m-1)\cdot\Delta_{n-1}
.
Further properties of this complex are discussed in
Appendix

A
.
By the proof of
2.1
, we have that

\mu\left(\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})\cap\Sigma(m,l^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime})\right)=\left(\Delta_{n-l,n}+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}\right)\cap\left(\Delta_{n-l^{\prime},n}+\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{1}\right).

Therefore, the faces of
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
encode the intersection of the distinct Grassmannian components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
.
Fig.

4
depicts this hypersimplicial complex for the case
n=2
.

Example 2.4
.

For
n=3
, the parameter
l
can be either be
1
or
2
. In the case
l=2
, we get that
\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
is a partition of
m+1
. This leads to components of the form
\Sigma(m,2,\mathbf{u})\simeq\mathrm{Gr}(2,3)=(\mathbb{P}^{2})^{*}
. In Plücker coordinates the moment map
\mu_{\mathbf{u},2}
of these components is

\begin{array}[]{cccl}\mu_{\mathbf{u},2}:&\Sigma(m,2,\mathbf{u})\simeq(\mathbb{P}^{2})^{*}&\longrightarrow&(m-1)\Delta_{1,3}+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}\\
&[a_{23},a_{13},a_{12}]&\longrightarrow&\left(\frac{|a_{23}|^{2}}{|a_{23}|^{2}+|a_{13}|^{2}+|a_{12}|^{2}},\frac{|a_{13}|^{2}}{|a_{23}|^{2}+|a_{13}|^{2}+|a_{12}|^{2}},\frac{|a_{12}|^{2}}{|a_{23}|^{2}+|a_{13}|^{2}+|a_{12}|^{2}}\right)+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}\par\end{array}

The image of
\mu_{\mathbf{u},2}
is the triangle defined by the vertices
(u_{1},u_{2}-1,u_{3}-1)
,
(u_{1}-1,u_{2},u_{3}-1)
and

.

For
l=1
,
\mathbf{u}
is a partition of
m
and we get components of the form
\Sigma(m,1,\mathbf{u})\simeq\mathrm{Gr}(1,3)=\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Since
\mathbf{u}
can not have zero entries, these type of components only appear for
m\geq 3
. In Plücker coordinates, the moment map
\mu_{\mathbf{u},1}
of these components is

\begin{array}[]{cccl}\mu_{\mathbf{u},1}:&\Sigma(m,1,\mathbf{u})\simeq\mathbb{P}^{2}&\longrightarrow&(m-1)\Delta_{2,3}+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}\\
&[a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}]&\longrightarrow&\left(\frac{|a_{2}|^{2}+|a_{3}|^{2}}{|a_{1}|^{2}+|a_{2}|^{2}+|a_{3}|^{2}},\frac{|a_{1}|^{2}+|a_{3}|^{2}}{|a_{1}|^{2}+|a_{2}|^{2}+|a_{3}|^{2}},\frac{|a_{1}|^{2}+|a_{2}|^{2}}{|a_{1}|^{2}+|a_{2}|^{2}+|a_{3}|^{2}}\right)+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}.\par\end{array}

\Delta_{2,4}+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}

.

.

\mathcal{K}^{[3]}_{4}

.

For instance, for
m=3
there is no component of the form
\Sigma(3,1,\mathbf{u})
, there is a component of the form
\Sigma(3,2,\mathbf{1})\simeq\mathrm{Gr}(2,4)
and there are four components of the form
\Sigma(3,3,\mathbf{u})
for
\mathbf{u}=(2,1,1,1),\ldots,(1,1,1,2)
.
Fig.

7
illustrates the hypersimplicial complex
\mathcal{K}_{4}^{[3]}
subdividing
2\Delta_{3}
. The maximal faces of the complex are the octahedron
\Delta_{2,4}
in purple and the simplices
\Delta_{3}+\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{3}+\mathbf{e}_{4}
in blue. As illustrated in
Fig.

7
, the gluing of the distinct components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{3}_{0}(X_{4})
is done through projective planes. In
\mathrm{Gr}(2,4)
, these projective planes correspond to the
2
–dimensional linear subspaces in Equation (
3
). In the components isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{3}
, the gluing is done through the torus-invariant projective planes.

For
m=4
,
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{4}_{0}(X_{4})
has
15
irreducible components. One of them is
\Sigma(4,1,\mathbf{1})\simeq\mathbb{P}^{3}
. Four of them are of the form
\Sigma(4,2,\mathbf{u})\simeq\mathrm{Gr}(2,4)
for
\mathbf{u}=(2,1,1,1),\ldots,(1,1,1,2)
. Finally, there are
10
irreducible components of the form
\Sigma(4,3,\mathbf{u})\simeq\mathbb{P}^{3}
where
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j}
for
1\leq i\leq j\leq 4
. The hypersimplicial complex
\mathcal{K}_{4}^{[4]}
encoding the intersection of these components is illustrated in
Fig.

8
. The blue tetrahedron corresponds to the images of through the moment map of the components of the form
\Sigma(4,3,\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j})
. The four green octahedrons correspond to the images of the components
\Sigma(4,2,\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{e}_{i}
. Finally, the purple tetrahedron corresponds to the image of the component
\Sigma(4,1,\mathbf{1})
.

Figure 8
.
Hypersimplicial complex
\mathcal{K}^{[4]}_{4}
encoding the intersection of the irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{4}_{0}(X_{4})
.

The faces of the hypersimplicial complex
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
are characterized in
Appendix

A
. The
(n-r)
–faces may be described as follows. Let
\max\{n-m+1,1\}\leq l\leq n-1
,
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{n}
with
|\mathbf{u}|=m+l-1
, and let

S_{1}
and
S_{2}
be two disjoint subsets of
[n]
with
|S_{1}|+|S_{2}|=r-1
and
l\leq|S_{1}|
and
|S_{2}|\leq n-l
. Then, the codimension
r
faces of
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
are of the form

(14)

\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}(S_{1},S_{2},l,\mathbf{u})\!:=\!\mathrm{Conv}(\mathbf{e}_{i_{1}}\!+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{i_{n-l-|S_{2}|}}\!:i_{1},\ldots,i_{n-l-|S_{2}|}\in[n]\setminus(S_{1}\sqcup S_{2})\text{ distinct})\!+\!\!\sum_{i\in S_{2}}\!\mathbf{e}_{i}\!+\!\mathbf{u}\!-\!\mathbf{1}\\
=\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}(S_{1},S_{2},l,\mathbf{u})=\left\{\sum_{i\not\in S_{1}\sqcup S_{2}}\lambda_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i}+\sum_{i\in S_{2}}\mathbf{e}_{i}:0\leq\lambda_{i}\leq 1\text{ and }\sum_{i\not\in S_{1}\sqcup S_{2}}\lambda_{i}=n-l-|S_{2}|\right\}+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}.\end{array}

Note that that the face
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}(S_{1},S_{2},l,\mathbf{u})
is obtained by setting
\lambda_{i}=0
for
i\in S_{1}
and
\lambda_{i}=1
for
i\in S_{2}
.
The ideals
[J]
in
\mathrm{Hilb}_{0}^{m}(X_{n})
lying on the face
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}(S_{1},S_{2},l,\mathbf{u})
are of the form

(15)

J=\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}}:i\in S_{1}\rangle+\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}+1}:i\in S_{2}\rangle+\langle g_{1},\ldots,g_{l-|S_{1}|}\rangle,

where
g_{1},\ldots,g_{l-|S_{1}|}
are linearly independent polynomials in
\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}}:i\in[n]\setminus(S_{1}\cup S_{2})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
.
Geometrically, these ideals form a Grassmannian
\mathrm{Gr}(l-|S_{1}|,n-r+1)
.
Note that the ideals in (
15
) are of the same form as those described in
1.11
. In particular, we have that

\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle\mu\left(\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})\cap\Sigma(m,l^{\prime},\mathbf{v})\right)=\left(\Delta_{n-l,n}+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}\right)\cap\left(\Delta_{n-l^{\prime},n}+\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{1}\right)=\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}(\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},1),\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},-1),l,\mathbf{u})\\
\\
\displaystyle=\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}(\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},-1),\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},-1),l^{\prime},\mathbf{v}).\end{array}

We conclude that the hypersimplicial complex
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
encodes the geometry of irreducible components of
\mathrm{Hilb}_{0}^{m}(X_{n})
: these components correspond to the Grassmannians associated with hypersimplices, and their intersections are likewise recorded. However,
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
cannot describe whether the intersection of these components is transversal or not. In
Section

4
, this question will be addressed. To do so, we associate to
the hypersimplicial complex
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
the variety
\mathcal{G}_{n}^{m}

defined as follows. For
\max\{1,n-m+1\}\leq l\leq n-1
and
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
with
|\mathbf{u}|=m+l-1
, we consider the Grassmannian
\mathrm{Gr}(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}})
(see
Eq.

2
). Recall that
\mathrm{Gr}(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}})
is isomorphic to
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
via the map
\varphi_{l,\mathbf{u}}
(see
Eq.

2
and
1.10
). Using this map, we can consider the equivalence relation in the disjoint union

(16)

\bigsqcup_{l=\max\{1,n-m+1\}}^{n-1}\bigsqcup_{{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{n}\\
|\mathbf{u}|=m+l-1\end{array}}}\mathrm{Gr}(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}})

given by
[E]\sim[E^{\prime}]
for
[E]\in\mathrm{Gr}(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}})

and
[E^{\prime}]\in\mathrm{Gr}(l^{\prime},\Lambda_{\mathbf{v}})
if and only if
\varphi_{l,\mathbf{u}}([E])=\varphi_{l^{\prime},\mathbf{v}}([E^{\prime}])
.
This condition occurs only in the intersection of
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
and
\Sigma(m,l^{\prime},\mathbf{v})
, which by
1.12
is a Grassmannian. The variety
\mathcal{G}_{n}^{m}
is the variety obtained by quotienting (
16
) by this equivalence relation. In other words,
\mathcal{G}_{n}^{m}
is obtained by gluing the Grassmannians
\mathrm{Gr}(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}})
via smaller Grassmannians. Since the intersections of the Grassmannians
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
are described by
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
, we obtain that
\mathcal{G}_{n}^{m}
is obtained by gluing the Grassmannians
\mathrm{Gr}(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}})
via the smaller Grassmannians corresponding to the faces of
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
. Formally, this gluing may be done iteratively. In (
16
) we first glue together the points that corresponds to the same vertex in
\mathcal{K}^{[m]}_{n}
. Then, we glue the lines that correspond to the same edges of
\mathcal{K}^{[m]}_{n}
. Inductively on the dimension of the faces, we glue together the subgrassmannians that corresponds to the same face of the complex.

Example 2.6
.

Fix
n=3
. For
m=2
, the hypersimplicial complex
\mathcal{K}_{3}^{[2]}
coincides with
\Delta_{1,3}
, and hence,
\mathcal{G}_{3}^{2}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{2}
. For
m=3
,
\mathcal{K}_{3}^{[3]}
has four
2
–dimensional hypersimplices as shown in
2.4
. These hypersimplices are
\Delta_{2,3}
and
\Delta_{1,3}+\mathbf{e}_{i}
for
i\in[3]
, which are depicted in
Fig.

5
. The variety
\mathcal{G}_{3}^{3}
is obtained by gluing
4
copies of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
following the intersections of the corresponding hypersimplices in
\mathcal{K}_{3}^{[3]}
: at each of the three
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{3}
–invariants lines of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
we glue a copy of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
through one of its invariant lines. Similarly, for
m=4
,
\mathcal{G}_{3}^{4}
is obtained by gluing
9
copies of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
through torus invariant lines following the hypersimplicial complex
\mathcal{K}_{3}^{[4]}
depicted in
Fig.

5
.

Example 2.7
.

Fix
n=4
. For
m=2
, the hypersimplicial complex
\mathcal{K}_{4}^{[2]}
coincides with
\Delta_{1,4}
, and hence,
\mathcal{G}_{4}^{2}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{3}
. For
m=3
,
\mathcal{K}_{4}^{[3]}
has five
3
dimensional hypersimplices as shown in
2.7
. These hypersimplices are
\Delta_{2,4}
and
\Delta_{1,4}+\mathbf{e}_{i}
for
i\in[4]
, which are depicted in
Fig.

7
. The variety
\mathcal{G}_{4}^{3}
is obtained by gluing
4
copies of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
to
\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}\nolimits(2,\langle x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}})\simeq\mathrm{Gr}(2,4)

following the intersections of the corresponding hypersimplices in
\mathcal{K}_{4}^{[3]}
(see
Fig.

7
). The hypersimplex
\Delta_{2,4}
has eight
2
–dimensional faces isomorphic to the simplex
\Delta_{2}
. Four of these faces coincide with a faces of each of the hypersimplices
\Delta_{1,4}+\mathbf{e}_{i}
for
i\in[4]
. Geometrically, the associated Grassmannian
\mathrm{Gr}(2,4)
has eight
\mathbb{P}^{2}
embedded which are of the form:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}Y_{i}:=\displaystyle\left\{[E]\in\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}\nolimits(2,4):E\subseteq\langle x_{j}:j\neq i\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}\right\}&\text{ and }&\displaystyle Y_{i}^{*}:=\left\{[E]\in\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}\nolimits(2,4):\langle x_{i}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq E\right\},\end{array}

for
i\in[4]
. The ideals associated to
Y_{i}
are of the form
\langle x_{i}^{2},f_{1},f_{2}\rangle
for
f_{1},f_{2}\in\langle x_{j}:j\neq i\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
, and the ideals associated to
Y_{i}^{*}
are of the form
\langle x_{i},f\rangle
for
f\in\langle x_{j}:j\neq i\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
. The varieties
Y_{i}
correspond to the four faces
\Delta_{2,4}(\emptyset,\{i\},2,\mathbf{1})
of
\Delta_{2,4}
that intersect with the hypersimplices
\Delta_{1,4}+\mathbf{e}_{i}
. For each
i\in[4]
we glue
\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}\nolimits(2,4)
and
\mathbb{P}^{3}
through
Y_{i}
and a toric invariant plane of
\mathbb{P}^{3}
. The variety obtained by this glue is
\mathcal{G}_{4}^{3}
.

Figure 9
.
Hypersimplicial complex
\mathcal{K}_{3,1}^{[4]}
and
\mathcal{K}_{3,2}^{[4]}.

For
\max\{1,n-m+1\}\leq l\leq n-1
, we consider the subvariety
\mathcal{G}_{l,n}^{m}
of
\mathcal{G}_{n}^{m}
given only by the components of
\mathcal{G}_{n}^{m}
that are Grassmannians of the form
\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}\nolimits(l,n)
. Analogously, we consider the hypersimplicial complex
\mathcal{K}_{n-l,n}^{[m]}
, which is the hypersimplicial subcomplex of
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
given by the hypersimplices of the form
\Delta_{n-l,n}+\mathbf{u}
. We refer to
Appendix

A
for further details on
\mathcal{K}_{n-l,n}^{[m]}
. The variety
\mathcal{G}_{l,n}^{m}
is the variety obtained by gluing the Grassmannian
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
via the faces of
\mathcal{K}_{n-l,n}^{[m]}
.

Example 2.8
.

The variety
\mathcal{G}_{2,3}^{4}
consists of
6
copies of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
that are glued together via torus invariant points as in the vertices of the hypersimplicial complex
\mathcal{K}_{1,3}^{[4]}
illustated in
Fig.

9
. Similarly,
\mathcal{G}_{1,3}^{4}
is obtained by taking
3
copies of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and gluing together a torus invariant point on each of them. The corresponding hypersimplicial complex is
\mathcal{K}_{2,3}^{[4]}
illustrated in
Fig.

9
.

Example 2.9
.

Fig.

10
depicts the hypersimplicial complexes
\mathcal{K}_{1,4}^{[3]}
and
\mathcal{K}_{2,4}^{[4]}
. These two complexes represent the varieties
\mathcal{G}_{1,3}^{3}
and
\mathcal{G}_{2,4}^{4}
. The variety
\mathcal{G}_{1,3}^{3}
consists of
4
copies of
\mathbb{P}^{3}
glued together via invariant torus points. Similarly,
\mathcal{G}_{2,4}^{4}
consists of
4
copies of
\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}\nolimits(2,4)
that are glued together via torus invariant lines.

Figure 10
.
Hypersimplicial complexes
\mathcal{K}_{1,4}^{[3]}
and
\mathcal{K}_{2,4}^{[4]}
.

A.8
described the intersection of
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
with a linear subspace of the form

(17)

H(S,\mathbf{a}):=\{\lambda_{i}=a_{i}:i\in S\}\ \text{ for }S\subseteq[n]\text{ and }\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}\text{ with }|\mathbf{a}|\leq m-1.

Such intersection is isomorphic to the hypersimplicial complex
\mathcal{K}_{n-|S|}^{[m-|a|]}
. On the levels of ideals,

\mu([J])
for
[J]\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
is contained in

H(S,\mathbf{a})
if and only if
x_{i}^{a_{i}+1}
for
i\in S
among its minimal generators. Therefore, the intersection of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
with this condition is isomorphic to
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m-\sum a_{i}}(X_{n-|S|})
. This intersection coincides with the image of the map

(18)

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\iota_{S,\mathbf{a}}&\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m-|\mathbf{a}|}(X_{n-|S|})&\longrightarrow&\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})\\
&[J]&\longmapsto&\left[J+\langle x_{i}^{a_{i}+1}:i\in S\rangle\right]\end{array}

which is an isomorphism onto its image. Moreover, such a map fits in the following commutative diagram

(19)

where the vertical maps are the moment map and
the map below is the translation by
\sum_{i\in S}a_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i}
.

Figure 11
.
Smoothable faces of
\mathcal{K}_{3}^{[2]}
,
\mathcal{K}_{3}^{[3]}
and
\mathcal{K}_{3}^{[4]}

Now, we describe the ideals in
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
that are mapped to a smoothable face of
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
via the moment map. We recall that a face
\Gamma
of
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
is smoothable face (see
A.9
) if one of the following conditions is satisfied

•

n=0
or
n=1
.

•

The face
\Gamma
is contained in
\Delta_{n-1,n}+\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{1}
for certain
\mathbf{v}
.

•

The face
\Gamma
is contained in a linear subspace
H(S,\mathbf{a})
as in (
17
), and in the intersection of
H(S,\mathbf{a})
and
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
, the face
\Gamma
is smoothable.

In
Appendix

A
equivalent definitions of smoothable faces are given.
Fig.

11
illustrates the smoothable faces for
\mathcal{K}_{3}^{[m]}
for
m=2,3,4
. The smoothable faces of
\mathcal{K}_{4}^{[m]}
for
m=3,4
are illustrated in
Fig.

12
.

Figure 12
.
Smoothable faces of the complexes
\mathcal{K}_{4}^{[3]}
and
\mathcal{K}_{4}^{[4]}
.

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
[J]\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
, then,
\mu([J])
lies in a smoothable face of
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
if and only if
J=\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}}:i\in S\rangle+\langle f\rangle
for
S\subset[n]
,
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{n}
with
|\mathbf{u}|=m+|S|
, and
f\in\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}}:i\not\in S\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

Assume first that
J=\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}}:i\in S\rangle+\langle f\rangle
and consider the integer vectors
\mathbf{u}_{S}=\sum_{i\in S}u_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i}
and
\mathbf{u}_{[n]\setminus S}=\sum_{i\not\in S}u_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i}
. Consider the ideal
J^{\prime}=\langle f\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}[x_{i}:i\not\in S]
. Then,
J^{\prime}
is a length
|\mathbf{u}_{[n]\setminus S}|
and via the map (
25
), we get that
[J]=\iota_{S,\mathbf{u}_{S}}([J^{\prime}])
. Now,
[J^{\prime}]\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{|\mathbf{u}_{[n]\setminus S}|}(X_{n-|S|})
is contained in the Grassmannian
\Sigma(|\mathbf{u}_{[n]\setminus S}|,1,\mathbf{u}_{[n]\setminus S})
. Therefore,
\mu([J^{\prime}])
lies in
\Delta_{n-1,n}+\mathbf{u}_{[n]\setminus S}-\mathbf{1}
, and hence,
\mu([J^{\prime}])
is contained in a smoothable face. Using the commutative diagram (
19
), we deduce that
\mu([J])
is contained in a smoothable face.

Assume now that
\mu([J])
lies in a smoothable face
\Gamma
. Then,
\Gamma
is a hypersimplex of the form
\Delta_{n^{\prime}-1,n^{\prime}}
for
n^{\prime}\leq n
(see
Appendix

A
). By the commutative diagram (
19
), it is enough to check the case when
n^{\prime}=n
. In other words,
\Gamma=\Delta_{n-1,n}+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}
.
In this case,
\mu([J])
lies in
\Gamma
if and only if
[J]\in\Sigma(m,1,\mathbf{u})
. The proof follows from the fact that any ideal in
\Sigma(m,1,\mathbf{u})
is of the form
\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}+1}:i\in S\rangle+\langle f\rangle
for
S\subsetneq[n]
and
f\in\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}}:i\not\in S\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
.
∎

Analogously to the notion of smoothable face, the notion of singular face is introduced in
Appendix

A
, which we recall here for convenience: A face
\Gamma
of
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
is
singular
if one of the following conditions is satisfied:

•

The face
\Gamma
is in the intersection of two distinct maximal faces.

•

The face
\Gamma
is smoothable of dimension at most
n-2
, i.e. at most codimension
1
.

In the following Proposition we describe the ideals that are contained in a singular face via the moment map.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
[J]\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
. Then
\mu([J])
is contained in a singular face if and only if one of the following conditions is satisfied

•

J
admits a minimal generator of the form
x_{i}^{u_{i}}
for
u_{i}\geq 2
.

•

J
admits a minimal representation of the form
\langle f,x_{i}:i\in S\rangle
for
f\in\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}};i\not\in S\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\emptyset\subsetneq S\subset[n]
.

Proof.

Let
[J]\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
lying in the component
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
.
By
2.10
,
\mu([J])
lies in a smoothable face if and only if
J=\langle f,x_{i}:i\in S\rangle
for
f\in\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}};i\not\in S\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
and
S\subset[n]
. Moreover, such a face has dimension
n-1
if and only if
S=\emptyset
. Therefore, the second condition of the definition of singular face corresponds to the second condition in
2.11
.

Assume now that
J
contains a generator of the form
x_{i}^{u_{i}}
with
u_{i}\geq 2
. Then
[J]
also lies in the Grassmannian
\Sigma(m,r+|S|-1,\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{e}_{i})
. Therefore,
\mu([J])
is singular, since it lies in the intersection of the hypersimplices corresponding to these two Grassmannians. Now assume that
\mu([J])
is contained in the intersection of two distinct hypersimplices
\Delta_{l,n}+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}
and
\Delta_{l^{\prime},n}+\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{1}
. Then,
[J]
is contained in the intersection of the Grassmannians
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
and
\Sigma(m,l^{\prime},\mathbf{v})
.
By
1.11
,
J
is as in (
7
). Since
\mathbf{u}\neq\mathbf{v}
, either
\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},1)
or
\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},-1)
is nonempty. Without loss of generality assume that
\kappa(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v},1)\neq\emptyset
. Then, there exists
i\in[n]
such that
x_{i}^{u_{i}}=x_{i}^{v_{i}+1}
is a minimal generator of
J
. Since
v_{i}\geq 1
, we deduce that
J
admits a minimal generators of the form
x_{i}^{u_{i}}
with
u_{i}\geq 2
.
∎

In
Section

5
and
Section

6
, we relate the notions of smoothable and singular faces with the smoothable ideals and the singular locus of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
.

3.
From the punctual to the global Hilbert scheme

With the notation and definitions of the previous sections, we will study the relation behind the combinatorics and the geometry of the Hilbert scheme
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
. From this interplay we will get all the irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
.

For
2\leq m^{\prime}\leq\min\{m,n-1\}
,
l=n+1-m^{\prime}
, and for
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}
with
|\mathbf{u}|=m-m^{\prime}
, consider the isomorphism

(20)

\phi_{m^{\prime},\mathbf{u}}:\Sigma(m^{\prime},l,\mathbf{1})\rightarrow\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{1})

given by the composition

\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}\Sigma(m^{\prime},l,\mathbf{1})&\overset{\varphi_{l,\mathbf{1}}^{-1}}{\longrightarrow}&\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}\nolimits(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{1}})&\longrightarrow&\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}\nolimits(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{1}})&\overset{\varphi_{l,\mathbf{u}}}{\longrightarrow}&\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{1}),\end{array}

where the middle map is induced by the isomorphism of vector spaces
\Lambda_{\mathbf{1}}\rightarrow\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{1}}
that sends
x_{i}
to
x_{i}^{u_{i}+1}
.
In other words, for
[J]\in\Sigma(m^{\prime},l,\mathbf{1})
, its image
\phi_{m^{\prime},\mathbf{u}}([J])
is the ideal whose generators are obtained by replacing
x_{i}
by
x_{i}^{u_{i}+1}
. Moreover,
the following diagram commutes

\hbox to184.87pt{\vbox to56.48pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 92.43282pt\lower-26.827pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-92.43282pt}{-19.65477pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-12.66667pt}{-23.84088pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
