\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

In particular, the map
\phi_{m^{\prime},\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}}
is the geometric analogous to the translation
+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}
in the definition of the moment map.

Proposition 3.1
.

For
2\leq m^{\prime}\leq\min\{m,n-1\}
and for
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}
with
|\mathbf{u}|=m-m^{\prime}
, consider the rational map

(21)

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\displaystyle\Sigma(m^{\prime},n+1-m^{\prime},\mathbf{1})\times\prod_{i\in[n]}\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}\nolimits^{u_{i}}L_{i}&\dashrightarrow&\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})\\
([J],q_{1},\ldots,q_{n})&\longmapsto&\mathbb{V}(J)\cup q_{1}\cup\cdots\cup q_{n}\end{array}.

Let
[J]\in\Sigma(m^{\prime},n+1-m^{\prime},\mathbf{1})
and let
Z
be a one parameter family in the domain of (
21
) that contains the point
z_{0}=([J],u_{1}\cdot\mathbf{0},\ldots,u_{n}\cdot\mathbf{0})
and the domain of definition of (
21
) intersect
Z
in a dense open subset. Then,
the restriction of the map (
21
) to
Z
can be uniquely extended to
z_{0}
and the image of
z_{0}
is
\phi_{m^{\prime},\mathbf{u}}([J])
, which does not depend on the family
Z
.

Proof.

Let
z=([J],q_{1},\ldots,q_{n})\in Z
lying in the domain of definition of (
21
). In particular,
q_{i}
represents
u_{i}
points in
L_{i}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
. Let
I_{i}=\langle x_{j}:j\neq i\rangle+\langle f_{i}\rangle
be the ideal of
q_{i}
. In other words, we can write
f_{i}
as
f_{i}=1+f_{i}^{\prime}
where
f_{i}^{\prime}
is a degree
u_{i}
polynomial in
x_{i}
with
f_{i}^{\prime}(0)=0
. Then, the ideal of
q_{1}\cup\cdots\cup q_{n}
in
R_{n}
is
\langle f_{1}\cdots f_{n}\rangle=\langle 1+\sum f_{j}^{\prime}\rangle
. Let
g_{1},\ldots,g_{n+1-m^{\prime}}
be the generators of
J
, where
g_{i}=a_{i,1}x_{1}+\ldots,a_{i,n}x_{n}
. Therefore, the ideal of
\mathbb{V}(J)\cup q_{1}\cup\cdots\cup q_{n}
is generated by

\langle g_{1}\left(1+\sum f_{j}\right),\ldots,g_{n+1-m^{\prime}}\left(1+\sum f_{j}\right)\rangle.

Now, we have that

g_{i}\left(1+\sum_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}\right)=g_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{i,j}x_{j}f_{j}^{\prime}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{i,j}x_{j}+a_{i,j}x_{j}f_{j}^{\prime}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{i,j}x_{j}(1+f_{j}^{\prime})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{i,j}x_{j}f_{j}.

Now, consider any limit of the form
\underset{z\rightarrow z_{0}}{\lim}z
. In other words, we are taking the limit when
f_{j}
goes to
x_{j}^{u_{j}}
for all
j\in[n]
.
Then, the image of such a limit is a length
m
ideal that must contained the ideal generated by

\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{i,j}x_{j}^{u_{j}+1}\text{ for all }i\in[n].

Note that this ideal is exactly
\phi_{m^{\prime},\mathbf{u}}([J])
. In particular,
the length of this ideal is
|\mathbf{u}|+n+1-(n+1-m^{\prime})=m
, and we conclude that
\phi_{m^{\prime},\mathbf{u}}([J])
is the image of the limit.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

3.1
allows us to relate the combinatorics studied in
Section

2
and the geometry of the Hilbert scheme. Mainly, it shows the relation between the map (
21
) and the translations made in the definition of the moment map and in the hypersimplices of
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
. Consider the translation by
\mathbf{u}
between the hypersimplices
\Delta_{m^{\prime}-1,n}
and
\Delta_{m^{\prime}-1,n}+\mathbf{u}
for
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}
and
|\mathbf{u}|=m-m^{\prime}
. Let
[J]\in\Sigma(m^{\prime},n+1-m^{\prime},\mathbf{1})
, then
\mu([J])
is in
\Delta_{m^{\prime}-1,n}
. The translation
\mu([J])+\mathbf{u}
can be interpreted geometrically as follows. Let
Z
be the length
m
ideal obtained by adding
u_{i}
nonzero points in the line
L_{i}
to the scheme
\mathbb{V}(J)
for all
i
. By
3.1
, collapsing all these nonzero points to the singularity leads to the length
m
ideal
\phi_{m^{\prime},\mathbf{u}}([J])
in
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
. The image via the moment map of this ideal is exactly
\mu([J])+\mathbf{u}
. Thus, the translation in the definition of the moment map is interpreted geometrically as adding to
\mathbb{V}(J)
extra nonzero points in the lines of
X_{n}
and collapsing them to the singularity.
This relation is explored in more detail in
Section

6.1
.

We now calculate the irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
. The strategy is to use
3.1
to distinguish which irreducible components of the punctual Hilbert scheme
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
lift to an elementary component of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
. To do so, we first introduce the candidates to irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
.
For
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{n}
with
|\mathbf{u}|=m+l-1
, we define the rational map

(22)

X_{n}\times\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})\dashrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m+1}(X_{n})

that sends a point
q\in X_{n}
and an ideal
[J]\in\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})

to the length-
m
subscheme
\{q\}\cup\mathbb{V}(J)
. The map (
22
) is not defined in
\mathbf{0}\times\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
. As a consequence, the image of (
22
) is not a closed subvariety.
3.1
allows us to extend (
22
) to a well-defined map. The image of the base locus is the union of the Grassmannians
\Sigma(m+1,l,\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{e}_{i})
for
i\in[n]
. In general, for
2\leq m^{\prime}\leq\min\{n-1,m\}
, we define the map

(23)

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathrm{sm}}^{m-m^{\prime}}(X_{n}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\})\times\Sigma(m^{\prime},n+1-m^{\prime},\mathbf{1})&\longrightarrow&\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})\\
(Y,[J])&\longmapsto&Y\cup\mathbb{V}(J).\end{array}

The extension of (
23
) to the closure of its domain will be studied in
Section

6.1
.
We denote the closure of the image of (
23
) by
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m,m^{\prime}}(X_{n})
. In other words,
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m,m^{\prime}}(X_{n})
is the reduced version of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m,m^{\prime}}(X_{n})
, defined as the closure of the locus of points
[J]\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
such that there exists
J_{0}
in the primary decomposition of
J
supported at
\mathbf{0}
with
[J_{0}]\in\Sigma(m^{\prime},n+1-m^{\prime},\mathbf{1})
.
The following result describes which irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
lift to elementary components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
and which not. This allows us to compute the irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
.

Theorem 3.3
.

Fix
n\geq 1
and
m\geq 1
. Let
l\in[n-1]
and let
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{n}
be partition of
m+l-1
.

(1)

If
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{1}=(1,\ldots,1)
and
m=n
, then
l=1
and
\Sigma(n,1,\mathbf{1})
is contained in
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X_{n})
.

(2)

If
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{1}=(1,\ldots,1)
and
2\leq m\leq n-1
, then
2\leq l=n+1-m\leq n-1
. In this case,
\Sigma(m,n+1-m,\mathbf{1})
is an irreducible component of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
. In particular,
\Sigma(m,n+1-m,\mathbf{1})
is an elementary component of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
.

(3)

If there exists
1\leq i\leq n
such that
u_{i}\geq 2
then
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
is contained in the closure of the image of the map

(24)

(L_{i}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\})\times\Sigma(m-1,l,\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{e}_{I})\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})

sending a pair
(q,[I])\in(L_{i}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\})\times\Sigma(n,l,\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{e}_{I})
to the length
m
scheme
\{q\}\cup\mathbb{V}(I)
. In particular,
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
is not an elementary component of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
.

Therefore, the irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
are either an irreducible component of the smoothable component or an irreducible component of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m,m^{\prime}}(X_{n})
for
2\leq m^{\prime}\leq\min\{m,n-1\}
.

Proof.

Assume first that
u_{i}\geq 2
for some
1\leq i\leq n
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
u_{1}\geq 2
.
Let
J
be a generic ideal in
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
. Then
J
is generated by
l
polynomials
f_{1},\ldots,f_{l}
of the form

f_{i}=a_{i,1}x_{1}^{u_{1}}+\cdots+a_{i,n}x_{n}^{u_{n}}.

We can assume that
a_{1,1}=1
. Replacing
f_{i}
by
a_{1,1}f_{i}-a_{i,1}f_{1}
for
2\leq i\leq l
, we can also assume that
a_{i,1}=0
for
2\leq i\leq l
. We consider the ideal

J_{\lambda}=\langle f_{1}+\lambda x_{1}^{u_{1}-1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{l}\rangle

for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
. Note that
J_{0}=J
and
J_{\lambda}
is a length
m
ideal in
R_{n}
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
.
As in Proposition
3.1
, for
\lambda\neq 0
, we may write
J_{\lambda}
as

J_{\lambda}=\langle(\lambda+x_{1})(x_{1}^{u_{1}-1}+\lambda^{-1}a_{1,2}x_{2}^{u_{2}}+\cdots+\lambda^{-1}a_{1,n}x_{2}^{u_{n}}),(\lambda+x_{1})f_{2},\ldots,(\lambda+x_{1})f_{n}\rangle.

We deduce that for
\lambda\neq 0
,
\mathbb{V}(J_{\lambda})
is the union of the point
(-\lambda,0,\ldots,0)
and a length
m-1
subscheme in
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{e}_{1})
given by the ideal

J^{\prime}_{\lambda}=\langle x_{1}^{u_{1}-1}+\lambda^{-1}a_{1,2}x_{2}^{u_{2}}+\cdots+\lambda^{-1}a_{1,n}x_{2}^{u_{n}},f_{2},\ldots,f_{n}\rangle.

Therefore, for
\lambda\neq 0
,
[J_{\lambda}]
is the image via (
24
) of the tuple
((-\lambda,0\ldots,0),[J^{\prime}_{\lambda}])
. By
3.1
, we deduce that
[J]
lies in the closure of the image of the map (
24
). Since
[J]
is a generic element of
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
, we conclude that
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
is also contained in this closure.

Now, assume that
l=1
,
m=n
and
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{1}
.
In this case, ideals in
\Sigma(n,1,\mathbf{1})
are generated by a linear form, so they correspond to hyperplanes passing through
\mathbf{0}
. Let
J
be a generic element in
\Sigma(n,1,\mathbf{1})
and let
H
be the corresponding hyperplane. Moreover, let
v
be a generic vector supported at
\mathbf{0}
and consider the family of affine hyperplanes
H_{t}=H+tv
. Since
H
and
v
are generic, for
t\neq 0
,
H_{t}
intersects each of the lines
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}
in a point distinct than
\mathbf{0}
. In particular,
H_{t}\cap X_{n}
consists of
n
distinct points in
X_{n}
and we obtain a family of length
n
schemes
H_{t}\cap X_{n}
that are smoothable for
t\neq 0
. We conclude that the scheme
H_{0}\cap X_{n}=\mathbb{V}(J)
is smoothable.

Assume that
2\leq m\leq n-1
,
\mathbf{u}=(1,\ldots,1)
and
l=n+1-m
. This implies that
n\geq 3
. We show that
\Sigma(m,n+1-m,\mathbf{1})
is an irreducible component of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
. For
m=2
, we saw that
\Sigma(2,n-1,\mathbf{1})
is an irreducible component of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{2}(X_{n})
in
1.3
since the tangent star is properly contained in the tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{\mathbf{0}}X_{n}
. Now assume that
m\geq 3
. For
0\leq k\leq m
, we consider the map

(25)

\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{k}(X_{n}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\})\times\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m-k}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})\dashrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})

sending
k
distinct points
\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{k}\}
in
X_{n}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
and a length
m-k
scheme
Z
supported at
\mathbf{0}
to
\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{k}\}\cup Z
. We denote the closure of the image of this map by
Y^{m}(n,k)
. Note that
Y^{m}(n,m)
is the smoothable component of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
.

To check that
\Sigma(m,n+1-m,\mathbf{1})
is an elementary component of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
it is enough to show that it is not contained in
Y^{m}(n,k)
for all
1\leq k\leq m
. We argue by contradiction as follows. Let
[J]
be a generic element in
\Sigma(m,n+1-m,\mathbf{1})
and assume that
[J]
lies in
Y^{m}(n,k)
for some
1\leq k\leq m
.
Note that generic ideals in
\Sigma(m,n+1-m,\mathbf{1})
are generated by
n+1-m
linear forms and they correspond to generic
(m-1)
–dimensional linear subspaces in
\mathbb{A}^{n}
passing through
\mathbf{0}
. Let
\Gamma
be the
(m-1)
–dimensional subspace associated to
J
. Then there exists an irreducible reduced curve
C
, a one–dimensional family of length
m
schemes
\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow C
and
t_{0}\in C
such that the fiber
\mathcal{Z}_{t_{0}}
is
\mathbb{V}(J)
and the fibers
Z_{t}
for
t\neq t_{0}
lie in the image of (
25
). Since
1\leq k
, there exists
1\leq i\leq n
such that
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
is contained in the image of the map

(L_{i}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\})\times\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{k-1}(X_{n}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\})\times\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m-k}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})\dashrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n}).

for
t\neq t_{0}
. Let
\Gamma_{t}
be the smallest linear subspace containing
Z_{t}
. Assume first that
1\leq k\leq m-1
. Since
k\leq m-1
,
Z_{t}
contains
\mathbf{0}
and a point in
L_{i}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
for
t\neq t_{0}
. Therefore,
\Gamma_{t}
is contained in
L_{i}
for
t\neq 0
. We deduce that
\Gamma_{0}=\Gamma
contains
L_{i}
. This is a contradiction since
\Gamma
is a generic linear subspace of dimension
m-1
containing
\mathbf{0}
. Next, assume that
[J]
lies in
Y^{m}(n,m)
, i.e., assume that
J
is smoothable. Then,
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
consists of
m
distinct points for
t\neq t_{0}
. Moreover, there exists
i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}
such that
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
is contained in the image of

(L_{i_{1}}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\})\times\cdots\times(L_{i_{m}}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\})\dashrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})

for
t\neq 0
.
Let
M_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}
be the affine subspace expand by
L_{i_{1}},\ldots,L_{i_{k}}
. Then
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
is contained in
M_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}
for any
t\in C
. Therefore,
\Gamma
is contained in
M_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}
. Since
m\leq n-1
, we have that
M_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}\subsetneq\mathbb{A}^{n}
. We deduce that if
J
is smoothable,
\Gamma
would be contained in

\bigcup_{1\leq i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\leq n}M_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}\subsetneq\mathbb{A}^{n}.

This is a contradiction since
J
and
\Gamma
are generic.

By the first statement of the theorem, we deduce that for
m\geq n
,
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
does not have any elementary component. Similarly, the second statament of the theorem implies that for
2\leq m\leq n-1
, the only elementary component of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
is
\Sigma(m,n+1-m,\mathbf{1})
. Therefore, we conclude that the irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
are the smoothable components of the irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m,m^{\prime}}(X_{n})
for
2\leq m^{\prime}\leq\min\{m,n-1\}
.
∎

The main consequences of
3.3
are the following results.

Corollary 3.4
.

The number of irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
is

\binom{m+n-1}{m}+\sum_{m^{\prime}=2}^{\min\{m,n-1\}}\binom{m-m^{\prime}+n-1}{m-m^{\prime}}.

Proof.

By
3.3
, the irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
are the irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X_{n})
and the irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m,m^{\prime}}(X_{n})
for
2\leq m^{\prime}\leq\min\{n-1,m\}
. The irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X_{n})
are given by the possible distribution of
m
distinct points among the
n
of
X_{n}
. Therefore,
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X_{n})
has
\binom{m+n-1}{m}
components. Similarly, the components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m,m^{\prime}}(X_{n})
are in correspondence with the components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m-m^{\prime}}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X_{n})
via the map (
23
). We deduce that the number of irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m,m^{\prime}}(X_{n})
is
\binom{m-m^{\prime}+n-1}{m-m^{\prime}}
.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

The number of irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m,m^{\prime}}(X_{n})
is
\binom{m-m^{\prime}+n-1}{m-m^{\prime}}
. These components are birational to

\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}\nolimits^{u_{1}-1}(L_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}\nolimits^{u_{n}-1}(L_{n})\times\Sigma(m^{\prime},n+1-m^{\prime},\mathbf{1}),

where
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{\geq 1}
with
|\mathbf{u}|=m-m^{\prime}+n
.
These components are in bijection with the hypersimplices of the form
\Delta_{m^{\prime}-1,n}+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{1}
in
\mathcal{K}^{[m]}_{n}
.

Corollary 3.6
.

Let
C
be an irreducible curve whose only singularity is a rational
n
-fold singularity. Then, the number of irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
is
\min\{n-1,m\}
. Moreover, these irreducible components are
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathrm{sm}}(C)
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m,m^{\prime}}(C)
for
2\leq m^{\prime}\leq\min\{n-1,m\}
, which are birational to

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}\nolimits^{m}(C)&\text{or}&\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}\nolimits^{m-m^{\prime}}(C)\times\mathrm{Gr}(n+1-m^{\prime},n)\,\,\,\text{ for }2\leq m^{\prime}\leq\min\{n-1,m\}.\end{array}

Proof.

Follows from
3.3
, since in this case the smoothable component is irreducible.
∎

Remark 3.7
.

To the best of our knowledge, this is the first example of Hilbert schemes of
m
points where the number of irreducible components initially increases and then remains constant as
m
varies. The graph of the number of irreducible components for
n
fixed as
m
varies is illustrated in
Fig.

1
.

Example 3.8
.

Let
C
be an irreducible curve whose unique singularity is a rational
3
–fold singularity. The number of irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
is
\mathrm{min}\{2,m\}.
Assuming that
m\geq 2
, the two irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
are the smoothable component and
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m,2}(C)
, which is birational to
\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}\nolimits^{m-2}(C)
.

Example 3.9
.

Let
C
be an irreducible curve whose unique singularity is a rational
4
–fold singularity. The number of irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
is
\mathrm{min}\{3,m\}.
For
m=2
, the irreducible components are the smoothable component
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{2}(C_{\mathrm{sm}})
and a
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{2,2}(C)\simeq\mathbb{P}^{3}
. For
m\geq 3
, the three irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
are
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{3}(C_{\mathrm{sm}})\simeq_{\mathrm{bir}}\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
,
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m,2}(C)\simeq_{\mathrm{bir}}\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}\nolimits^{m-2}C\times\mathbb{P}^{3}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m,3}(C)\simeq_{\mathrm{bir}}\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}\nolimits^{m-3}C\times\mathrm{Gr}(2,4)
. Note that two of the first two irreducible components have dimension
m
, while the third irreducible component has dimension
m+1
.

4.
The local Hilbert scheme and its schematic structure

In the previous sections we have carried out a study of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
from the perspective of algebraic varieties, and not considering the possibility of a non–reduced structure. This is reflected in
3.3
where the reduced structure of the irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
is given. The next goal is to analyse the reducedness of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
. We use the notation and classical results from deformation theory as presented in
[
25
]
, to which we refer the reader for further details. Consider the local Hilbert scheme
H_{J}^{n}
of
\mathbb{V}(J)
in
X_{n}
. This is defined as the functor

\begin{array}[]{cccc}H_{J}^{n}:&\mathcal{Art}&\longrightarrow&\text{Sets}\\
&A&\longmapsto&\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{ Deformations of }\mathbb{V}(J)\text{ in }X_{n}\\
\text{ over }A\end{array}\right\}.\end{array}

Then
H_{J}^{n}
is prorepresented by
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n}),[J]}
. We compute
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n}),[J]}
by calculating the complete local ring prorepresenting the local Hilbert scheme using the same strategy as
[
23
]
.
We carry out this computation for the most singular points of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
, which are the ideals
J
such that
\mu_{m}(J)
is a vertex of
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
. In other words,
J=\langle x_{1}^{u_{1}},\ldots,x_{n}^{u_{n}}\rangle
. Up to labeling of the variables, we may assume
J=\langle x_{1}^{u_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}^{u_{k}},x_{k+1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
, where
u_{i}\geq 2
for
i\leq k
. Note that
k\geq 1
, since for
k=0
, the ideal
J
has length
1
. The value of
k
has the following combinatorial meaning. The hypersimplicial complex
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
fills the simplex
(m-1)\Delta_{n-1}
, which it can also be seen as a simplicial complex. Then,
k-1
is the dimension of the face of
(m-1)\Delta_{n-1}
where
\mu([J])
lies. For instance, if
k=1
, then
\mu([J])
is a vertex of
(m-1)\Delta_{n-1}
. If
k=n
, then
\mu([J])
is an interior point of
(m-1)\Delta_{n-1}
.

Consider the ring

S_{k}=\mathbb{C}\left[A_{1},\ldots,A_{n},\alpha_{i,j,l}:i\in[n],j\in[k]\text{ and }l\in[u_{j}-1]\,\right]

and the ideal of
S_{k}
given by

(26)

\begin{array}[]{ll}J_{k}=&\langle A_{i}A_{j}:\text{ for }k+1\leq j\leq n\text{ and }i\in[n]\setminus\{j\}\rangle\\
&+\langle A_{i}\alpha_{j,r,s}:\text{ for }k+1\leq j\leq n,i\in[n]\setminus\{j\},r\in[k]\text{ and }s\in[u_{r}-1]\rangle\\
&+\langle\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}\alpha_{j,j,1}-A_{i}:\text{ for }j\in[k]\text{ and }i\in[n]\setminus\{j\}\rangle\\
&+\langle\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}A_{j}:\text{ for }j\in[k]\text{ and }i\in[n]\setminus\{j\}\rangle\\
&+\langle\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}\alpha_{j,j,l+1}-\alpha_{i,j,l}:\text{ for }j\in[k],i\in[n]\setminus\{j\}\text{ and }l\in[u_{j}-2]\rangle\\
&+\langle\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}\alpha_{j,r,l}:\text{ for }j\in[k],i\in[n]\setminus\{j\},r\in[k]\setminus\{j\}\text{ and }l\in[u_{j}-1]\rangle.\\
\end{array}

Using this ideal we compute
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n}),[J]}
in the following:

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
0\leq k\leq n
and let
\mathbf{u}=\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{n}
such that
|\mathbf{u}|=m+n-1
,
u_{i}\geq 2
for
1\leq i\leq k
and
u_{i}=1
for
k+1\leq i\leq n
. Then, the local Hilbert scheme of

J=\langle x_{1}^{u_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}^{u_{k}},x_{k+1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
in
X_{n}
is prorepresented by the completion of the quotient
S_{k}/J_{k}
localized at the origin.

Proof.

Let
S\in\mathcal{A}
be a local Artinian
\mathbb{C}
–algebra with residue field
\mathbb{C}
, and let
\mathfrak{m}_{S}
its maximal ideal.
Let
R_{S}:=R\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}S
.
A deformation of
\mathbb{V}(J)
in
\mathrm{Spec}(R_{n})
over
\mathrm{Spec}(S)
is an ideal
J_{S}
of
R_{S}
such that
R_{S}/J_{S}
is flat over
S
and
(R_{S}/J_{S})\otimes_{S}\mathbb{C}=R/J
. In other words,
J_{S}
is generated by

\begin{array}[]{ccl}\displaystyle f_{1}&=&x_{1}^{u_{1}}+A_{1}+f_{1,1}(x_{1})+\cdots+f_{1,n}(x_{n}),\\
&\vdots&\\
\displaystyle f_{k}&=&x_{k}^{u_{k}}+A_{k}+f_{k,1}(x_{1})+\cdots+f_{1,n}(x_{n}),\\
\displaystyle f_{k+1}&=&x_{k+1}+A_{k+1}+f_{k+1,1}(x_{1})+\cdots+f_{k+1,n}(x_{n}),\\
&\vdots&\\
\displaystyle f_{n}&=&x_{n}+A_{n}+f_{n,1}(x_{1})+\cdots+f_{n,n}(x_{n}),\\
\end{array}

where
A_{i}
lies in
\mathfrak{m}_{S}
and
f_{i,j}(x_{j})
is a polynomial in
x_{j}
with coefficients in
\mathfrak{m}_{S}
and with no independent coefficient.
By
[
27
,
Tag 051G
]
,
R_{S}/J_{S}
is a free
S
-module of rank
m
. By Nakayama’s Lemma,
R_{S}/J_{S}
is freely generated by

1,x_{1},\ldots,x_{1}^{u_{1}-1},\cdots,x_{k},\ldots,x_{k}^{u_{k}-1}.

We can write
x_{i}^{d}
with
d_{i}\geq u_{i}
as a linear combination of
1,x_{1},\ldots,x_{1}^{u_{1}-1},\cdots,x_{k},\ldots,x_{k}^{u_{k}-1}
with coefficients in
S
. Therefore, we may assume that the degree of
f_{i,j}
is at most
u_{j}-1
. In particular,
f_{i,j}=0
for
k+1\leq j\leq n
and we get

f_{i,j}=\sum_{l=1}^{u_{j}-1}\alpha_{i,j,l}x_{j}^{l}

for
1\leq j\leq k
. We deduce that
J_{S}
is generated by

(27)

\begin{array}[]{ccl}\displaystyle f_{1}&=&\displaystyle x_{1}^{u_{1}}+A_{1}+\sum_{l=1}^{u_{1}-1}\alpha_{1,1,l}x_{1}^{l}+\cdots+\sum_{l=1}^{u_{k}-1}\alpha_{1,k,l}x_{k}^{l},\\
&\vdots&\\
\displaystyle f_{k}&=&\displaystyle x_{k}^{u_{k}}+A_{k}+\sum_{l=1}^{u_{1}-1}\alpha_{k,1,l}x_{1}^{l}+\cdots+\sum_{l=1}^{u_{k}-1}\alpha_{k,k,l}x_{k}^{l},\\
\displaystyle f_{k+1}&=&\displaystyle x_{k+1}+A_{k+1}+\sum_{l=1}^{u_{1}-1}\alpha_{k+1,1,l}x_{1}^{l}+\cdots+\sum_{l=1}^{u_{k}-1}\alpha_{k+1,k,l}x_{k}^{l},\\
&\vdots&\\
\displaystyle f_{n}&=&\displaystyle x_{n}+A_{n}+\sum_{l=1}^{u_{1}-1}\alpha_{n,1,l}x_{1}^{l}+\cdots+\sum_{l=1}^{u_{k}-1}\alpha_{n,k,l}x_{k}^{l}.\\
\end{array}

For
k+1\leq j\leq n
and
i\in[n]\setminus\{j\}
,we consider in
R_{S}/J_{S}
the relation

(28)

\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle 0=A_{i}f_{j}-x_{j}f_{i}=A_{i}\left(x_{j}+A_{j}+\sum_{l=1}^{u_{1}-1}\alpha_{j,1,l}x_{1}^{l}+\cdots+\sum_{l=1}^{u_{k}-1}\alpha_{j,k,l}x_{k}^{l}\right)-A_{i}x_{j}=\\
\displaystyle A_{i}A_{j}+\sum_{l=1}^{u_{1}-1}A_{i}\alpha_{j,1,l}x_{1}^{l}+\cdots+\sum_{l=1}^{u_{k}-1}A_{i}\alpha_{j,k,l}x_{k}^{l}.\end{array}

Note that (
28
) is a relation among the free generators of
R_{S}/J_{S}
. We deduce that the coefficients of (
28
) vanish in
S
, and we get that for
1\leq i\leq n
and
k+1\leq j\neq i\leq n

(29)

\begin{array}[]{ll}A_{i}A_{j}=0&\\
A_{i}\alpha_{j,a,b}=0&\text{ for }1\leq a\leq k\text{ and }1\leq b\leq u_{a}-1.\end{array}

Similarly, for
1\leq j\leq k
and
i\in[n]\setminus\{j\}
, we consider in
R_{S}/J_{S}
the relation

(30)

\begin{array}[]{c}0\!=\!\displaystyle\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}f_{j}-x_{j}f_{i}=\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}\left(\!x_{j}^{u_{j}}+A_{j}+\!\sum_{l=1}^{u_{1}-1}\alpha_{j,1,l}x_{1}^{l}+\cdots+\sum_{l=1}^{u_{k}-1}\alpha_{j,k,l}x_{k}^{l}\!\right)\!-\!A_{i}x_{j}\!-\!\sum_{l=1}^{u_{j}-1}\alpha_{i,j,l}x_{j}^{l+1}\\
=\displaystyle(\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}\alpha_{j,j,1}-A_{i})x_{j}+\!\sum_{l=1}^{u_{j}-2}(\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}\alpha_{j,j,l+1}-\alpha_{i,j,l})x_{j}^{l+1}+\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}A_{j}+\!\sum_{a\neq j}\sum_{l=1}^{u_{a}-1}\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}\alpha_{j,a,l}x_{a}^{l}.\par\end{array}

Again, (
30
) is a relation among the free generators of
R_{S}/J_{S}
. We deduce that for
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq k
with
i\neq j
we have

(31)

\begin{array}[]{ll}\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}\alpha_{j,j,1}-A_{i}=0&\\
\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}A_{j}=0&\\
\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}\alpha_{j,j,l+1}-\alpha_{i,j,l}=0&\text{ for }1\leq l\leq u_{j}-2\\
\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}\alpha_{j,a,l}=0&\text{ for }1\leq a\neq j\leq k,\text{ and }1\leq l\leq u_{a}-1.\end{array}

Therefore, if the
R_{S}/J_{S}
is a deformation over
S
, then the coefficients of
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
satisfy equations (
29
) and (
31
). Conversely, assume that the coefficients of
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
satisfy equations (
29
) and (
31
). Then,
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
satisfy the relations (
28
) and (
30
). Modulo
S/\mathfrak{m}_{s}\simeq\mathbb{C}
, these relations are exactly

x_{i}x_{j}^{u_{j}}=0

for
i\neq j
, which are exactly the syzygies of
J
. By
[
25
, Corollary A.11]
,
R_{S}/J_{S}
is flat over
S
and we conclude that
R_{S}/J_{S}
is a deformation over
S
. Therefore, the local Hilbert scheme of
[J]
is prorepresented by the completion of the stalk at the origin of the quotient of

S_{k}=\mathbb{C}[A_{1},\ldots,A_{n},\alpha_{i,j,l}:1\leq i\leq n,\,\,1\leq j\leq k,\,\,1\leq l\leq u_{j}-1]

by the ideal generated by the relations in (
29
) and (
31
), which coincide with
J_{k}
.
∎

4.1
computes the stalk
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n}),[J]}
as the completion of the quotient
S_{k}/J_{k}
. However, the representation of this quotient is quite complicated. In
Appendix

B
we give a better representation of this ring. In particular,
B.3
and
B.8
together with
B.2
compute the irreducible components of the stalk of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
at the point
[J]=[\langle x_{1}^{u_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}^{u_{k}},x_{k+1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle]
. Now, we identify each of these components with the corresponding components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
.

Proposition 4.2
.

For
k=1
,
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n}),[J]}
has
n+1
irreducible components and they correspond to the ideals in
B.3
. A generic point in the component corresponding to
\langle A_{1},\alpha_{1,1}\rangle
represents a length
m
scheme that is a point in

(32)

\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m-2}(L_{1})\times\Sigma(2,n-1,\mathbf{1}).

A generic point in the component associated to
\langle\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\rangle
represents a smoothable length
m
scheme which is a point in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(L_{1})
. A generic point in
\langle A_{1},\alpha_{j}:2\leq j\leq n\text{ and }j\neq i\rangle
for
2\leq i\leq n
represents a smoothable length
m
scheme that
is a point in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m-1}(L_{1})\times L_{i}
.

Proof.

Let
K
be an ideal in the irreducible component corresponding to the ideal
\langle\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\rangle
. We write the generators of
K
as in (
27
). Via the isomorphism in
B.2
, we deduce that
A_{2}=\cdots=A_{n}=0
and
\alpha_{2,1,u_{1}-1}=\cdots=\alpha_{n,1,u_{1}-1}=0
. Using the ideal (
26
) we deduce that the generators of
K
are

\begin{array}[]{cll}f_{1}&=&\displaystyle A_{1}+\sum_{l=1}^{u_{1}-1}\alpha_{1,1,l}x_{1}^{l}+x_{1}^{u_{1}},\\
f_{2}&=&x_{2},\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
f_{n}&=&x_{n}.\end{array}

Hence,
K
represents a length
m
ideal in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(L_{1})
. Assume now that
K
corresponds to a point in the ideal
\langle A_{1},\alpha_{j}:2\leq j\leq n\text{ and }j\neq i\rangle
for
2\leq i\leq n
. As before, pulling back these conditions via the isomorphism build in
B.2
, we deduce that the generators of
K
are of the form

\begin{array}[]{cll}f_{1}&=&\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{u_{1}-1}\alpha_{1,1,l}x_{1}^{l}+x_{1}^{u_{1}}=x_{1}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{u_{1}-2}\alpha_{1,1,l+1}x_{1}^{l}+x_{1}^{u_{1}-1}\right),\\
f_{2}&=&x_{2},\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
f_{i}&=&\displaystyle x_{i}+\alpha_{i,1,u_{1}-1}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{u_{1}-2}\alpha_{1,1,l+1}x_{1}^{l}+x_{1}^{u_{1}-1}\right),\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
f_{n}&=&x_{n}.\end{array}

We deduce that the primary decomposition of
K
consists on the ideals
\langle\sum_{l=0}^{u_{1}-2}\alpha_{1,1,l+1}x_{1}^{l}+x_{1}^{u_{1}-1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
, which represents a point in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m-1}(L_{1})
and
\langle x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{i}+\alpha_{i,1,u_{1}-1}\alpha_{1,1,1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
, which represents a point in
L_{i}
. Similarly, if
K
is in the component corresponding to
\langle A_{1},\alpha_{1,1}\rangle
, the generators of
K
are

\begin{array}[]{cll}f_{1}&=&\displaystyle\sum_{l=2}^{u_{1}-1}\alpha_{1,1,l}x_{1}^{l}+x_{1}^{u_{1}}=x_{1}^{2}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{u_{1}-3}\alpha_{1,1,l+2}x_{1}^{l}+x_{1}^{u_{1}-2}\right),\\
f_{2}&=&\displaystyle x_{2}+\sum_{l=1}^{u_{1}-1}\alpha_{2,1,l}x_{1}^{l}=x_{2}+\alpha_{2,1,u_{1}-1}x_{1}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{u_{1}-3}\alpha_{1,1,l+2}x_{1}^{l}\right),\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
f_{n}&=&\displaystyle x_{n}+\sum_{l=1}^{u_{1}-1}\alpha_{n,1,l}x_{1}^{l}=x_{n}+\alpha_{n,1,u_{1}-1}x_{1}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{u_{1}-3}\alpha_{1,1,l+2}x_{1}^{l}\right).\end{array}

We deduce that the primary decomposition of
K
consists of two ideals. First, the ideal
\langle\sum_{l=0}^{u_{1}-3}\alpha_{1,1,l+2}x_{1}^{l},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
which leads to length
m-2
scheme in
L_{1}
. The second ideal is

\langle x_{1}^{2},x_{2}-\alpha_{2,1,u_{1}-1}\alpha_{1,1,2}x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}-\alpha_{n,1,u_{1}-1}\alpha_{1,1,2}x_{1}\rangle,

which represent a length
2
scheme in
\Sigma(2,n-1,\mathbf{1})
.
∎

Proposition 4.3
.

For
k\geq 2
, the number of irreducible components of
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n}),[J]}
is

(33)

n+\sum_{i=1}^{\min\{k,n-2\}}\binom{k}{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}n+2^{k}-1&\text{ if }k\leq n-2\\
n+2^{n-1}-2&\text{ if }k=n-1\\
2^{k}-2&\text{ if }k=n\end{array}\right.,

and each irreducible component corresponds to the ideals in
B.8
. A generic point in the components corresponding to
\mathcal{K}_{a}
represents length
m
schemes that are points in

\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{u_{a}}(L_{a})\times\prod_{i\in[n]\setminus\{a\}}\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{u_{i}-1}(L_{i}).

In particular, points in the components corresponding to
\mathcal{K}_{a}
are smoothable.
A generic point in the components corresponding to
\mathcal{J}_{S}
represents length
m
schemes that are points in

(34)

\prod_{i\in S}\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{u_{i}-2}(L_{i})\times\prod_{i\in[k]\setminus S}\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{u_{i}-1}(L_{i})\times\Sigma(|S|+1,n-|S|,\mathbf{1}).

Proof.

We proceed as in the proof of
4.2
. We use the isomorphism of
B.2
to translate the conditions imposed by the ideals in
B.8
to the ring
S_{k}/J_{k}
and the generators (
27
). Let
K
be an ideal in the irreducible component corresponding to the ideal
\mathcal{J}_{S}
for
S
as in
B.8
. We may write the generators of
K
as in (
27
). To simplify these generators, we consider the polynomials

f_{i}^{\prime}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle x_{i}^{u_{i}-2}+\sum_{l=2}^{u_{i}-1}\alpha_{i,i,l}x_{i}^{l-2}\,\,\,\text{ for }i\in S,\\
\displaystyle x_{i}^{u_{i}-1}+\sum_{l=1}^{u_{i}-1}\alpha_{i,i,l}x_{i}^{l-1}\,\,\,\text{ for }i\in[k]\setminus S.\\
\end{array}\right.

Modulo the pullback of
\mathcal{J}_{S}
via the isomorphism in
B.2
, we may write the generators of
K
as

\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle f_{i}=x_{i}^{u_{i}}+\sum_{l=2}^{u_{i}-1}\alpha_{i,i,l}x_{i}^{l}=x_{i}^{2}f_{i}^{\prime}&\text{ for }i\in S,\\
\displaystyle f_{i}=x_{i}^{u_{i}}+\sum_{l=1}^{u_{i}-1}\alpha_{i,i,l}x_{i}^{l}+\sum_{j\in S}\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}x_{j}f_{j}^{\prime}\par&\text{ for }i\in[k]\setminus S,\\
\displaystyle f_{i}=x_{i}+\sum_{j\in S}\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}x_{j}f_{j}^{\prime}\par&\text{ for }i\not\in[k].\end{array}

Now, we analyze the scheme defined by these equations. Assume first that
f_{i}^{\prime}=0
for some
i\in S
. This implies that
0=x_{j}f_{i}^{\prime}=\alpha_{i,i,2}x_{j}
for
j\neq i
. We get a component of
K
the form
\langle f_{i}^{\prime},x_{j}:j\neq i\rangle
for
i\in S
which represent a point in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{u_{i}-2}(L_{i})
. Assume on the contrary that
f_{i}^{\prime}\neq 0
for every
i\in S
. Then,
x_{i}^{2}=0
for every
i\in S
. The generators of
K
modulo this condition are

\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle f_{i}=x_{i}^{u_{i}}+\sum_{l=1}^{u_{i}-1}\alpha_{i,i,l}x_{i}^{l}+\sum_{j\in S}\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}\alpha_{i,i,2}x_{j}\par&\text{ for }i\in[k]\setminus S,\\
\displaystyle f_{i}=x_{i}+\sum_{j\in S}\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}\alpha_{i,i,2}x_{j}\par&\text{ for }i\not\in[k].\end{array}

For
i\in[k]\setminus S
we get that

0=x_{i}f_{i}=x_{i}^{2}f_{i}^{\prime}.

If
f_{i}^{\prime}=0
, then, multiplying by
x_{j}
for
j\neq i
we get that
x_{j}=0
for
j\neq i
. Therefore, we obtained the ideal
\langle f_{i}^{\prime},x_{j}:j\neq i\rangle
for
i\in[k]\setminus S
, which represent a point in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{u_{i}-1}(L_{i})
. If on the contrary
f_{i}^{\prime}\neq 0
for every
i\in[k]
. Since
x_{i}^{2}f_{i}^{\prime}=0
, we deduce that
x_{i}^{2}=0
for every
i\in[k]
. Therefore, we obtain the ideal

\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle\langle x_{i}^{2}:i\in[k]\rangle+\langle\alpha_{i,i,1}x_{i}+\sum_{j\in S}\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}\alpha_{i,i,2}x_{j}:i\in[k]\setminus S\rangle+\langle x_{i}+\sum_{j\in S}\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}\alpha_{i,i,2}x_{j}:i\not\in[k]\rangle\\
\displaystyle=\langle\alpha_{i,i,1}x_{i}+\sum_{j\in S}\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}\alpha_{i,i,2}x_{j}:i\in[k]\setminus S\rangle+\langle x_{i}+\sum_{j\in S}\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}\alpha_{i,i,2}x_{j}:i\not\in[k]\rangle,\end{array}

which represents a point in
\Sigma(|S|+1,n-|S|,\mathbf{1})
. We conclude that
K
defines a length
m
scheme which corresponds to a point in

\prod_{i\in S}\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{u_{i}-2}(L_{i})\times\prod_{i\in[k]\setminus S}\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{u_{i}-1}(L_{i})\times\Sigma(|S|+1,n-|S|,\mathbf{1}).

∎

We can relate
4.2
and
4.3
with the combinatorics in
Section

2
. For
k=1
, the ideal
J=\langle x_{1}^{m},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
is the intersection of the
n+1
irreducible components of the Hilbert scheme described in
4.2
.
The ideal
J
corresponds to the vertex
(m-1)e_{1}
of the simplex
(m-1)\Delta_{n-1}
. The only hypersimplex of the hypersimplicial complex
\mathcal{K}^{[m]}_{n}
containing this vertex is
\Delta_{1,n}+(m-2)e_{1}
.
The component of the form (
32
) corresponds to this translated hypersimplex. The translation by
(m-2)e_{1}
geometrically corresponds to the factor
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m-2}(L_{1})
of the irreducible components. The rest of the irreducible components containig the point
[J]
are smoothable and cannot be seen from the complex
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
. Therefore, these components do not come from the punctual Hilbert scheme. In particular, the number of irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
and
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
that contain
[J]
is different. Similarly, for
k\geq 2
, we can associate to the components of the form (
34
) corresponding to
\mathcal{J}_{S}
the hypersimplex
\Delta_{n,|S|}+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{e}_{S}-\mathbf{1}
. Such a hypersimplex corresponds to the component
\Sigma(m,n-|S|,\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{e}_{S})
of the punctual Hilbert scheme. This component is exactly the intersection of the punctual Hilbert scheme and the component (
34
). For
k=n-1
and
a\not\in[k]
, we can associate to the component corresponding to
\mathcal{K}_{a}
in
4.3
the hypersimplex
\Delta_{n-1,n}+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{e}_{[n]\setminus\{a\}}-\mathbf{1}
. Similarly, for
n=k
and
a\in[k]
, we can associate to the component corresponding to
\mathcal{K}_{a}
in
4.3
the hypersimplex
\Delta_{n-1,n}+\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{e}_{[n]\setminus\{a\}}-\mathbf{1}
.

Remark 4.4
.

Fix the ideal
J=\langle x_{1}^{u_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}^{u_{k}},x_{k+1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
with
u_{i}\geq 2
for
i\in[k]
and
|\mathbf{u}|=m+n-1
. Then,
\mu([J])=\mathbf{u}
is a vertex of
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
that is contained in the relative interior of a
(k-1)
–dimensional face of
(m-1)\cdot\Delta_{n-1}
. The number of hypersimplices containing the vertex
\mu
is exactly

2^{k}-1
if
k<n
and
2^{k}-2
if
k=n
. Therefore, this number is the number of irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
containing
[J]
. On the other hand,
by
4.2
and
4.3
, the number of irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
containing
[J]
is given by (
33
). For
k\leq n-1
, this number of irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
and
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
differs by
n
if
k\leq n-2
and by
n-1
if
k=n-1
. These extra components that do not appear in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
are described in
4.3
and they are smoothable irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
that contain
[J]
. For
k=n
, the number of irreducible components of the local and punctual Hilbert scheme coincides.

Now, we show the local structure of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
inside the stalk

\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n}),[J]}
.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
1\leq k\leq n
and let
\mathbf{u}=\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{n}
such that
|\mathbf{u}|=m+n-1
,
u_{i}\geq 2
for
1\leq i\leq k
and
u_{i}=1
for
k+1\leq i\leq n
. Then, the completion of the stalk of the punctual Hilbert scheme
at
J=\langle x_{1}^{u_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}^{u_{k}},x_{k+1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
, denoted by
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n}),[J]}

is isomorphic to the completion of the quotient

(35)

\frac{\mathbb{Z}[\alpha_{i,j}:i\in[n],j\in[k]\setminus\{i\}]}{\langle\alpha_{i,j}\alpha_{j,r}:j\in[k],\,i\in[n]\setminus\{i\},\,r\in[k]\setminus\{j\}\rangle}

localized at the origin. Through this isomorphism, the irreducible components of
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n}),[J]}
corresponds to the ideals of (
35
)

(36)

\mathcal{J}_{[k],T}:=\langle\alpha_{i,j}:i,j\in[k]\setminus T,\,i\neq j\rangle+\langle\alpha_{i,j}:j\in T\text{ and }i\in[n]\setminus\{j\}\rangle

for
T\subsetneq[k]
if
k\neq n
, or
\emptyset\neq T\subsetneq[k]
if
k=n
. Moreover,the ideal
\mathcal{J}_{[k],T}
represents the irreducible component

\Sigma(m,n-k+|T|,\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{e}_{[k]\setminus T})

of the punctual Hilbert scheme.

Proof.

By
4.1
,
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n}),[J]}
corresponds to the ideal of
S_{k}/I_{k}
describing the deformations of
\mathbb{V}(J)
supported at the origin.
Let
K
be an ideal corresponding to a point in
S_{k}/J_{k}
. In other words, the generators of
K
are given by (
27
). We need to check when the ideal
K
is an ideal supported at the origin. The polynomial
x_{i}f_{i}=x_{i}(x_{i}^{u_{i}}+A_{i}+\sum_{l=1}^{u_{i}-1}\alpha_{i,i,l}x_{i}^{l})
is a polynomial in
K
. If
K
is supported only at the origin, then
x_{i}=0
must be the only solution of
x_{i}f_{i}=0
. We deduce that
A_{i}=\alpha_{i,i,1}=\cdots=\alpha_{i,i,u_{i}-1}=0
.
Modulo this relation, we get that the generators of
K
are

f_{i}=x_{i}^{u_{i}}+\sum_{i\in[k]\setminus\{i\}}\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}x_{j}^{u_{j}-1},

where
\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}\alpha_{j,r,u_{r}-1}=0
for every
i\in[n]
,
j,r\in[k]\setminus\{i\}
and
j\neq r
. Together with the generators of
J_{k}
and identifying
\alpha_{i,j,u_{j}-1}
with
\alpha_{i,j}
we get the ring (
35
). The primary decomposition of ideal in (
35
) is given by
B.4
for
S=[k]
. Now fix an ideal
\mathcal{J}_{[k],T}
in the primary decomposition of (
35
). Modulo
\mathcal{J}_{[k],T}
, the generatos of the length
m
ideal
K
are

f_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle x_{i}^{u_{i}}&\text{ for }i\in[k]\setminus T,\\
\displaystyle x_{i}^{u_{i}}+\sum_{j\in[k]\setminus{T}}\alpha_{i,j}x_{j}^{u_{j}-1}&\text{ for }i\in T,\\
\displaystyle x_{i}^{u_{i}}+\sum_{j\in[k]\setminus{T}}\alpha_{i,j}x_{j}^{u_{j}-1}&\text{ for }i\not\in[k].\\
\end{array}\right.

Note that the generators
f_{i}
for
i\in[k]\setminus T
are not required since the are recovered from the multiplication
x_{i}f_{j}
for
j\not\in[k]\setminus T
.
We deduce that the ideals in the component corresponding to
\mathcal{J}_{[k],T}
are given by
n-k+|T|
linearly independent polynomials in the vector space

\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}}:i\in T\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}+\langle x_{i}^{u_{i}-1}:i\in[k]\setminus T\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}+\langle x_{i}:i\not\in[k]\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}.

Therefore, the component
\mathcal{J}_{[k],T}
correspond with the irreducible component

\Sigma(m,n-k+|T|,\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{e}_{[k]\setminus T})

of the punctual Hilbert scheme.
∎

4.5
allows us to carry out the local study of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
, for the reducedness of this scheme and the transversality of the intersection of the irreducible components. To derive these results, we need first the following lemmas.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
[J]\in\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
such that
\mu([J])
is a vertex of
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
. Then,
[J]
is a reduced point of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
and
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
.

Proof.

To show that
[J]
is a reduced point of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
, it is enough to show that the stalk
\mathcal{O}_{\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n}),[J]}
is reduced. By Lemma
[
27
, Lemma 07NZ]
, it is enough to check that the completion of this stalk is reduced. By
4.1
and
B.2
, this completion is the quotient
\mathcal{S}_{k}/\mathcal{J}_{k}
(see (
55
),(
56
) and (
57
) for the relevant definitions). Since the primary decomposition of the ideal
\mathcal{J}_{k}
calculated in
B.3
and
B.8
is given by prime ideals, the ideal
\mathcal{J}_{k}
is radical. Hence,
\mathcal{S}_{k}/\mathcal{J}_{k}
is a reduced ring.

Similarly, to show that
[J]
is a reduced point of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
, it is enough to check that the ring (
35
) is reduced. The primary decomposition of the ideal in (
35
) is given in Equation (
36
) in
4.5
. The proof follows from the fact that the ideals (
36
) are prime.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
[J]\in\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
, then there exists
[J_{0}]\in\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
in the closure of the
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{n}
–orbit of
[J]
such that
\mu([J_{0}])
is a vertex of
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
.

Proof.

The statement of the Lemma is independent of the schematic structure of the Hilbert scheme. Therefore, we can replace
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
by
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
. Assume first that
J
is supported at
\mathbf{0}
, i.e.
[J]\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
.
By
1.13
, it is enough to check the analogous statement for Grassmannians. Let
[E]\in\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}\nolimits(l,n)
be generated by the image of an
n\times l
matrix
A=(a_{i,j})
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
A
is a block matrix of the form

A=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{Id}_{l}\\
\hline\cr A^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}.

For
t=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{n}
,
t\cdot[E]
is the vector subspace generated by image of the matrix
t\cdot A=\mathrm{Diag}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\cdot A
. Therefore, taking the limit when
t_{l+1},\ldots,t_{n}
goes to zero we get the linear subspace
\langle\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{l}\rangle
, which is a torus invariant point. Therefore, it corresponds to a vertex of
\Delta_{l,n}
via the moment map, and it lies in the closure of the orbit of
[E]
.

Assume now that
[J]\not\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
.
Since the statement of the lemma holds for ideals in the punctual Hilbert scheme, it is enough to check that in the closure of the
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{n}
–orbit of
[J]
there is a point in the punctual Hilbert scheme.
Consider a one parameter family
\lambda:\mathbb{C}^{*}\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{n}
such that
the limit of
\lambda(t)
when
t
goes to
0
is
\mathbf{0}
. Then, for any point
q
in
X_{n}
, the limit of
\lambda(t)\cdot q
when
t
goes to
0
is the singularity
\mathbf{0}
. Therefore, the limit of
\lambda(t)\cdot[J]
when
t
goes to
0
is a length
m
ideal supported at
\mathbf{0}
. We conclude that in the closure of the
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{n}
–orbit of
[J]
there is a point in
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
.
∎

Remark 4.8
.

4.2
and
4.3
and
4.7
provide an alternative proof to
3.3
: Let
Z
be an elementary component of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
. By
4.7
,
Z
contains a point
[J]\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
corresponding to a vertex of
\mathcal{K}^{[m]}_{n}
. Since
Z
is an irreducible component of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
, it should correspond to an irreducible component of the completion of the stalk of
[J]
calculated in
4.1
.
4.2
and
4.3
give a geometrical interpretation to each of the irreducible components of this stalk. The only cases where these irreducible components are entirely contained in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
are exactly the ones described in
3.3
.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
C
be an irreducible curve whose singularities are all rational
n
–fold singularities. Then,
for
m\geq 2
, the Hilbert scheme
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
is Cohen-Macaulay if and only if
n\leq 3
.

Proof.

First, note that for
n\geq 4
, the Hilbert scheme
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
is not equidimensional, and hence, it is not Cohen-Macaulay. Assume that
n\leq 3
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
C=X_{n}
. Let
[J]\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
, we need to check that the completion of the stalk of
[J]
is Cohen-Macaulay.
By
4.7
, we may assume that
[J]\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
and
\mu([J])
is a vertex of
\mathcal{K}^{[m]}_{n}
. By
4.1
and
B.2
, it is enough to check that the ring
\mathcal{S}_{k}/\mathcal{J}_{k}
is Cohen-Macaulay for
n\leq 3
and
k\in[n]
. A computation in
Macaulay2

[
10
]
shows that
\mathcal{S}_{k}/\mathcal{J}_{k}
is Cohen-Macaulay for
n\leq 3
and
k\in[n]
.
∎

Theorem 4.10
.

The punctual Hilbert scheme
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
is reduced and isomorphic to
\mathcal{G}_{n}^{m}
.

Proof.

First, we prove that
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
is reduced. Assume on the contrary that

[J]
is a nonreduced point in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
. This implies that
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{n}
–orbit of
[J]
is nonreduced, and hence, the closure of the
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{n}
–orbit of
[J]
is nonreduced. Bby
4.7
, there exists a ideal
[J^{\prime}]
in the closure of the orbit such that
\mu([J^{\prime}])
is a vertex of
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
. This is a contradiction since by
4.6
,
[J^{\prime}]
is a reduced point.

We know check that
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{G}^{m}_{n}
. Note that by the universal property of pushouts, we have a map
\varphi:\mathcal{G}_{n}^{m}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
that on each component
\mathop{\mathrm{Gr}}\nolimits(l,\Lambda_{\mathbf{u}})
of
\mathcal{G}_{n}^{m}
it is the map
\varphi_{l,\mathbf{u}}
. Therefore,
\varphi
is injective and its restriction to each irreducible component of
\mathcal{G}_{n}^{m}
and
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
leads to an isomorphism. Therefore, to check that
\varphi
is an isomorphism, it is enough to check what happens at the intersections. In other words, we need to check that in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
the intersection of the Grassmannians
\Sigma(m,l,\mathbf{u})
is locally the intersection of affine spaces. By
4.7
and using the torus action, it is enough to check this condition around an ideal
[J]
corresponding to a vertex of
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
. Then the proof follows from
4.5
since all the components of the completion of the stalk at
[J]
are affine spaces.
∎

Using the same technique as in
4.10
we derive that
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
is reduced.

Theorem 4.11
.

The Hilbert scheme of points
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
is reduced.

Proof.

Let
[I]\in\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
be a nonreduced point. Then the
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{n}
–orbit of
[I]
is nonreduced, and hence, the closure of the
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{n}
–orbit is also nonreduced. By
4.7
, the closure of this orbit contains an ideal
[J]
in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{m}(X_{n})
associated to a vertex of
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{m}
via the moment map.
This is a contradiction since by
4.6
,
[J]
is a reduced point of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
. Thus, we conclude that
[I]
is a reduced point of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
.
∎

As a consequence of Theorems
4.10
and
4.11
, we have that
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})=\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})=\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
. Therefore, for the rest of the paper, we will use the notation
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
and
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
.
The following improvement of
3.6
where we no longer require to take the reduced structure follows from
4.11
together with
3.3
.

Corollary 4.12
.

Let
C
be an irreducible curve with a unique rational
n
-fold singularity. Then, the irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
are

\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathrm{sm}}^{m}(C)\text{ and }\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m,m^{\prime}}(C)\text{ for }2\leq m^{\prime}\leq\min\{m,n-1\}.

The number of irreducible components is
\min\{n-1,m\}
. Moreover, these irreducible components are birational to

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}\nolimits^{m}(C)&\text{or}&\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}\nolimits^{m-m^{\prime}}(C)\times\mathrm{Gr}(n+1-m^{\prime},n)\,\,\,\text{ for }2\leq m^{\prime}\leq\min\{m,n-1\}.\end{array}

We can generalize
4.12
to irreducible curves with several rational fold like singularities.
Given integers
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
m,n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}\in Z_{\geq 2}
, we define the number
\rho(k,m,n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
as the cardinality of the set

S(k,m,n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}):=\left\{\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{k}:|\mathbf{m}|\leq m,\,m_{i}\neq 1\text{ and }0\leq m_{i}\leq\min\{m,n_{i}-1\}\right\}.

Corollary 4.13
.

Let
C
be an irreducible curve whose singularities are
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\in C
where
p_{i}
is a rational
n_{i}
-fold singularity. Then, the number of irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
is
\rho(k,m,n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
. Moreover,
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
is reduced and its irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
are birational to

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}\nolimits^{m}(C)&\text{ and }&\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}\nolimits^{m-|\mathbf{m}|}(C)\times\prod_{i=1}^{k}\Sigma(m_{i},n_{i}+1-m_{i},\mathbf{1})\end{array}

for
\mathbf{m}\in S(k,m,n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
.

Proof.

We first show that
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
is reduced. Given a point
[J]\in\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
, we can decompose
\mathbb{V}(J)
as

\mathbb{V}(J)=\mathbb{V}(J_{0})\cup\mathbb{V}(J_{1})\cup\cdots\cup\mathbb{V}(J_{k})
where
J_{0}
is supported at the smooth locus of
C
and
J_{i}
is supported at
p_{i}
for every
i\in[k]
. Let
m_{i}
be the length of
J_{i}
.
Around
[J]
, étale locally
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
is isomorphic to the product
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m_{0}}_{\mathrm{sm}}(C)\times\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m_{1}}(C)\times\cdots\times\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m_{k}}(C)
for
([J_{0}],[J_{1}],\ldots,[J_{k}])
(cf.
[
13
]
). Note that
[J_{0}]
is reduced in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m_{0}}_{\mathrm{sm}}(C)
. The punctual Hilbert scheme
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m_{i}}_{p_{i}}(C)
is isomorphic to
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m_{i}}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n_{i}})
. Therefore, we may see
[J_{i}]
also as a point in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m_{i}}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n_{i}})\subset\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m_{i}}(X_{n_{i}})
through this isomorphism. Then,
the completion of the stalk of
[J_{i}]
in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m_{i}}(C)
is isomorphic to the completion of the stalk of
[J_{i}]
in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m_{i}}(X_{n_{i}})
which is reduced by
4.11
.

Next, we calculate the number of irreducible components. An elementary component of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
parametrizes length
m
subschemes supported at one fixed singular point
p_{i}
. Therefore, elementary components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
are in bijection with elementary components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n_{i}})
for
i\in[k]
. These components correspond to the vectors in
S(k,m,n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
of the form
m\mathbf{e}_{i}
. By (
23
) we obtain that the non-elementary components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
correspond to the closure of the image of the map

(37)

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\displaystyle\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m-|\mathbf{m}|}_{\mathrm{sm}}(C\setminus\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\})\times\prod_{i=1}^{k}\Sigma(m_{i},n_{i}+1-m_{i},\mathbf{1})&\longrightarrow&\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)\\
([J_{0}],[J_{1}],\dots,[J_{k}])&\longmapsto&\left[\mathbb{V}(J_{0})\cup\cdots\cup\mathbb{V}(J_{k})\right]\end{array}

for
\mathbf{m}\in S(k,m,n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
with
\mathbf{m}\neq m\mathbf{e}_{i}
and
i\in[k]
. Here,
\Sigma(m_{i},n_{i}+1-m_{i},\mathbf{1})
is seen as the corresponding elementary component in the punctual Hilbert scheme
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m_{i}}_{p_{i}}(C)
. Moreover, the map (
37
) is birational onto its image.
Note that for
\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{0}
, the corresponding component is the smoothable component. In particular, we conclude that the irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(C)
are in bijection with vectors in
S(k,m,n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
.
∎

Remark 4.14
.

One can derive a formula for the cardinality of
\rho(k,m,n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
. To do so, we introduce the number
\chi(k,m,n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
as the cardinality of the set

\{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{k}:0\leq|\mathbf{m}|\leq m\text{ and }0\leq m_{i}\leq n_{i}\}.

We set
\chi(0,m)=1
for
k=0
.
Using the Exclusion-Inclusion formula, one may check that

\chi(k,m,n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})=\displaystyle\sum_{J\subseteq[k]}(-1)^{|J|}\binom{m-\sum_{j\in J}(n_{j}+1)+k}{k}.

Decompose the set
S(k,m,n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
as

S=\bigsqcup_{J\subseteq[k]}S(k,m,n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})\cap\{m_{i}=0:i\not\in J\}\cap\{m_{i}\geq 2:i\in J\}.

For
J\subseteq[k]
, the cardinality of the corresponding set in the above disjoint union is

\chi(|J|,m-2,n_{i}-2:i\in J)
. We conclude that the number of irreducible components in
4.13
is

\rho(k,m,n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})=\displaystyle\sum_{J\subseteq[k]}\chi(|J|,m-2,n_{i}-2:i\in J)=\displaystyle\sum_{J\subseteq[k]}\displaystyle\sum_{I\subseteq J}(-1)^{|I|}\binom{m-2-\sum_{i\in I}(n_{i}-1)+|J|}{|J|}.

5.
Singularities

The goal of this section is to describe the singular locus of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
. For doing this, we will rely heavily in Combinatorics, in particular the hypersimplicial complex, as given in
Sections

2
and
A
.

5.1.
Singular Locus

We will first compute the singular locus of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
. By a classical result in deformation theory (cf.
[
25
]
), the dimension of the tangent space of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
at the point
[J]
is given by

\mathop{\mathrm{dim}}\nolimits_{\mathbb{C}}\mathrm{T}_{[J]}\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})=\mathop{\mathrm{dim}}\nolimits_{\mathbb{C}}\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(J,R/J).

To apply this formula, we need the syzygies of
J
, which are computed in
B.1
.
Using this lemma, we will describe the singular locus of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
by the combinatorics of
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
using the notion of singular face introduced in
Section

2
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
[J]\in\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

[J]
is a singular point of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
.

(2)

\mu_{m}([J])
lies in a singular face of
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
.

(3)

J
admits a minimal generator of the form
x_{i}^{u_{i}}
with
u_{i}\geq 2
or
J
is minimally generated by
\langle f,x_{i}:i\in S\rangle
for
f\in\langle x_{j}^{u_{j}}:j\not\in S\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\emptyset\subsetneq S\subset[n]
.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.11
, (2) and (3) are equivalent.
So it is enough to show that (1) and (3) are equivalent. By
1.4
there exists
1\leq l\leq n
and
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
such that
J
is minimally generated by
f_{1},\ldots,f_{l}
where

\begin{pmatrix}f_{1}\\
\vdots\\
f_{l}\end{pmatrix}=A\,\,\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}^{u_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
x_{n}^{u_{n}}\end{pmatrix}.

and
A
is a size
l\times n
matrix as in
1.4
. Every
\varphi\in\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(J,R/J)
is uniquely determined by
l
elements
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{l}\in R/J
satisfying the syzygies (
52
) of
J
, where
\alpha_{j}:=\varphi(f_{j})
for
1\leq j\leq l
. Write
\alpha_{j}
as follows

\alpha_{j}=\alpha_{j,0}+\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{n}\sum_{s=1}^{u_{r}}\alpha_{j,r,s}x_{r}^{s}.

For
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j<k\leq l
we have the following equalities:

(38)

\begin{array}[]{c}0=A_{k,i}x_{i}\alpha_{j}-A_{j,i}x_{i}\alpha_{k}=A_{k,i}\alpha_{j,0}x_{i}+\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{u_{i}-1}A_{k,i}\alpha_{j,i,s}x_{i}^{s+1}-\sum_{s=1}^{u_{i}-1}A_{j,i}\alpha_{k,i,s}x_{i}^{s+1}=\\
(A_{k,i}\alpha_{j,0}-A_{j,i}\alpha_{k,0})x_{i}+\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{u_{i}-1}(A_{k,i}\alpha_{j,i,s}-A_{j,i}\alpha_{k,i,s})x_{i}^{s+1}.\end{array}

We first assume that
J
does not admit a minimal generator of the form
x_{i}^{u_{i}}
. Then, none of the terms in (
38
) vanishes in
R/J
, and we
obtain that
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{l}
satisfy the relations

(39)

\begin{array}[]{ll}A_{k,i}\alpha_{j,0}-A_{j,i}\alpha_{k,0}=0&\text{ for }1\leq j<k\leq l\text{ and }1\leq i\leq n,\\
A_{k,i}\alpha_{j,i,s}-A_{j,i}\alpha_{k,i,s}=0&\text{ for }1\leq j<k\leq l,\text{ }1\leq i\leq n\text{ and }2\leq s\leq u_{i}.\end{array}

Rewrite the first type of relations in (
39
) as

(\alpha_{k,0},-\alpha_{j,0})\begin{pmatrix}A_{j,1}&\cdots&A_{j,n}\\
A_{k,1}&\cdots&A_{k,n}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

Since the matrix
A
has maximum rank we get that the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}A_{j,1}&\cdots&A_{j,n}\\
A_{k,1}&\cdots&A_{k,n}\end{pmatrix}

has rank
2
. This implies that
\alpha_{j,0}=0
for all
1\leq j\leq l
. The second type of relations in (
39
) can be written as the
2\times 2
minors of the matrix

(40)

\begin{pmatrix}A_{1,i}&\alpha_{1,i,s}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
A_{l,i}&\alpha_{l,i,s}\end{pmatrix}

for
1\leq i\leq n
and
2\leq s\leq u_{i}
.
Since
A
has no vanishing columns, there exists
1\leq j\leq l
such that
A_{j,i}
is nonzero. Thus, the
2\times 2
minors of the matrix (
40
) give
l-1
relations among
\alpha_{1,i,s},\ldots,\alpha_{l,i,s}
for
1\leq i\leq n
and
2\leq s\leq u_{i}
. Hence
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{l}
satisfy
l+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(u_{i}-1)(l-1)=l+(|\mathbf{u}|-n)(l-1)
relations. We conclude that the dimension of the tangent space at
[J]
is

l\,m-l-(|\mathbf{u}|-n)(l-1)=l\,m-l-(m+l-1-n)(l-1)=l(n-l)+(m+l-1-n).

On the other hand,
[J]
is contained in

\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m,n+1-l}(X_{n})
, which has dimension
l(n-l)+(m+l-1-n)
. Thus
[J]
is a smooth point of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
.

Now, assume that
J
admits a generator of the form
x_{r}^{u_{r}}
. If
u_{r}\geq 2
, then
J
is in the intersection of two irreducible components of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
and therefore
J
is singular a singular point of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
. Hence we can assume that
u_{r}=1
for every such generator.
Let
f_{1},\ldots,f_{a}
be the minimal generators of
J
that are not of the form
x_{r}
. In other words, the minimal generators of
J
are
f_{1},\ldots,f_{a},x_{n-l+a+1},\ldots,x_{n}
, and therefore we can write these generators as

\begin{pmatrix}f_{1}\\
\vdots\\
f_{a}\\
x_{n-l+a+1}\\
\vdots\\
x_{n}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pNiceArray}\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}^{u_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
x_{n-l+a}^{u_{n-l+a}}\\
x_{n-l+a+1}\\
\vdots\\
x_{n}\end{pmatrix},

where
A
has maximal rank and it has no vanishing row or column. Hence the relations among
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{l}
in
R/J
are

(41)

\begin{array}[]{ll}x_{i}\alpha_{j}=0&\text{ for }a+1\leq j\leq l\text{ and }i\neq n-l+j,\\
x_{i}\alpha_{j}=0&\text{ for }1\leq j\leq a\text{ and }n-l+a+1\leq i\leq n,\\
A_{k,i}x_{i}\alpha_{j}-A_{j,i}x_{i}\alpha_{k}=0&\text{ for }1\leq j<k\leq a\text{ and }1\leq i\leq n-l+a.\\
\end{array}

Since
x_{i}\in J
for
n-l+a+1\leq i\leq n
, the left-hand side of the second equation in (
41
) is zero in
R/J
and is therefore redundant. Similarly, the first relation is identically zero in
R/J
for
n-l+a+1\leq i\leq n
. We obtain

(42)

\begin{array}[]{ll}x_{i}\alpha_{j}=0&\text{ for }a+1\leq j\leq l\text{ and }1\leq i\leq n-l+a,\\
A_{k,i}x_{i}\alpha_{j}-A_{j,i}x_{i}\alpha_{k}=0&\text{ for }1\leq j<k\leq a\text{ and }1\leq i\leq n-l+a.\\
\end{array}

From the first equation in (
42
), we get that

\alpha_{j,0}=0\text{ and }\alpha_{j,i,s}=0\text{ for }a+1\leq j\leq l,\,\,1\leq i\leq n-l+a\text{ and }1\leq s\leq u_{i}-1.

In particular, there are
(l-a)(m+l-n)
linear equations on
\alpha_{a+1},\ldots,\alpha_{l}
. As in the first part of the proof, the number of linearly independent equations obtained from the second equations in (
42
) is

a\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{n-l+a}(a-1)(u_{i}-1)=a+(a-1)(m+l-n-1).

Now, assume first that
a=1
. In other words,
J
is satisfy condition (3) of
5.1
. Then, we have no equation of the second type in (
42
). In particular, the dimension of the tangent space is

ml-(l-1)(m+l-n)=l(n-l)+m+l-n.

On the other hand,
J
lies in

\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m,n+1-l}(X_{n})
, which has dimension
l(n-l)+m+l-n-1
. We conclude that
[J]
is a singular point of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
.

Assume now that
a\geq 2
. Then, the dimension of the tangent space at
[J]
is

ml-(l-a)(m+l-n)-a-(a-1)(m+l-n-1)=l(n-l)+m+l-n-1.

Moreover,
[J]
is contained in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m,n+1-l}(X_{n})
which has also dimension
l(n-l)+m+l-n-1
. We conclude that
[J]
is a smooth point of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
.
∎

5.1
characterizes the points in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
that are singular in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
. The study done in
Section

3
, allow us to move from the punctual Hilbert scheme to the global Hilbert scheme, giving a characterization of the singular locus of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
J
be a length
m
ideal of
R
and let
J_{1},\ldots,J_{k}
be its primary decomposition, where
J_{a}
has length
m_{a}
. Then,
[J]
is singular in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})

if and only if there exists
1\leq a\leq k
such that
[J_{a}]
is contained in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{u_{a}}_{0}(X_{n})
and
J_{a}
satisfies one of the conditions in
5.1
.

Proof.

Let
[J]\in\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
and let
J_{1},\ldots,J_{k}
be its primary decomposition. Let
Z
be the subscheme of
X_{n}
defined by
J
and let
Z_{i}
be the subscheme defined by
J_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k
.
Using
[
25
, Section 4.6.5]
, we get that

\mathrm{T}_{[J]}\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})=H^{0}(Z,N_{Z/X})=\bigoplus_{1\leq i\leq k}H^{0}(Z_{i},N_{Z_{i}/X})=\bigoplus_{1\leq i\leq k}\mathrm{T}_{[J_{i}]}\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n}).

The proof then follows from
5.1
.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

Using the notation in
5.2
, if
m_{a}=1
, then
[J_{a}]\in\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{0}}^{1}(X_{n})=\{p\}
, which is singular. In particular,
\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}\nolimits^{m-1}\left(X_{n}\setminus\{p\}\right)\times\{p\}
is a subset of the singular locus of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
.

5.2.
Local picture of the singularities

Next, we give a description of the singularities of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
. By
4.7
, the closure of any torus orbit in
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
contains a point
[\mathbb{V}(J)]\in\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_{n})
that corresponds to a vertex of
\mathcal{K}_{n}^{[m]}
via the moment map. Therefore, it is enough to describe the singularities of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
at points of the form
[J]=[\langle x_{1}^{u_{1}},\ldots,x_{k}^{u_{k}},x_{k+1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle]
for
1\leq k\leq n
and
u_{i}\geq 2
for
i\in[k]
.
Using
4.1
and
B.2
, it is enough to analyze the singularty at the origin of the variety defined by the ideal
\mathcal{J}_{k}
. By
B.3
,
B.8
and
B.7
, locally at the origin
\mathbb{V}(\mathcal{J}_{k})
is the union of normal toric varieties. We describe the singularity through the gluing of the polytopes associated to these toric varieties. To do so, we first do some simplification on the coordinate ring
\mathcal{S}_{k}/\mathcal{J}_{k}
. For
k=1
, the variables
\alpha_{1,2},\ldots,\alpha_{1,m-1}
do not appear in the ideal
\mathcal{J}_{1}
. Therefore, we have that

\mathcal{S}_{1}/\mathcal{J}_{1}\simeq\mathbb{C}[\alpha_{1,2},\ldots,\alpha_{1,m-1}]\otimes(\mathcal{S}_{1}^{\prime}/\mathcal{J}_{1}),

where
\mathcal{S}_{1}^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}[\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n},A_{1},\alpha_{1,1}]
is the polynomial ring in the rest of the variables.
Similarly, for
k\geq 2
, the variables
\beta_{1,s}
for
i\in[k]
and
2\leq s\leq u_{i}-1
do not appear in the ideal
\mathcal{J}_{K}
. Therefore, we have that

\mathcal{S}_{k}/\mathcal{J}_{k}\simeq\mathbb{C}[\beta_{i,j}:1\leq i\leq k\text{ and }2\leq j\leq u_{i}-1]\otimes(\mathcal{S}_{k}^{\prime}/\mathcal{J}_{k}),

where
\mathcal{S}_{k}^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}[\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k},\alpha_{i,j}:i\in[n],j\in[k],i\neq j]
is the polynomial ring in the rest of the variables. In particular, the singularity type of
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m}(X_{n})
at
[J]
is the same as the affine variety
\mathrm{spec}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{k}/\mathcal{J}_{k})
at the origin.

We start by the case
k=1
. In other words, assume that
J=\langle x_{1}^{m},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
and
\mu([J])
is an edge of
(m-1)\cdot\Delta_{n-1}
.
By
B.3
, the primary decomposition of
\mathcal{J}_{1}
is given by the ideals
J_{0}=\langle A_{1},\alpha_{1,1}\rangle
,
J_{1}=\langle\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\rangle
and
J_{i}=\langle A_{,}\alpha_{j}:2\leq j\leq n,\,j\neq i\rangle
. Note that
\mathbb{V}(J_{0})
is a linear subspace of dimension
n-1
, and
\mathbb{V}(J_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq n
is a linear subspace of dimension
2
. By
4.2
,
J_{0}
corresponds to the component
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m,2}(X_{n})
and
J_{i}
for
i\geq 1
correspond to the smoothable component.
Now,
\mathbb{V}(J_{0})
and
\mathbb{V}(J_{1})
intersect in the origin. Similarly,
\mathbb{V}(J_{0})
and
\mathbb{V}(J_{i})
for
2\leq i\leq n
intersect in the line corresponding to
\mathbb{C}[\alpha_{i}]
. Finally,
\mathbb{V}(J_{i})
and
\mathbb{V}(J_{j})
for
1\leq i<j\leq n
intersect in the line
\mathbb{C}[\alpha_{1,1}]
.
We can associate to
J_{0}
the simplex
\Delta_{n}
whose vertices are labeled by
v_{0},v_{2}\ldots,v_{n}
. Moreover, we associate to
J_{i}
the simplex
M_{i}:=\Delta_{2}
whose vertices are labeled by
w_{i,0}
,
w_{i,1}
and
w_{i,2}
. We construct the simplicial complex
\mathbb{S}_{1}
obtained by the following gluing:

•

For
2\leq i\leq n
, glue the edge
\overline{v_{0},v_{i}}
of
\Delta_{n}
with the edge
\overline{w_{i,0},w_{i,2}}
of
M_{i}
.

•

For
1\leq i<j\leq n
, glue the edge
\overline{w_{i,0},w_{i,1}}
of
M_{i}
with the edge
\overline{w_{j,0},w_{j,1}}
of
M_{j}
.

In
Fig.

13(a)
and
Fig.

13(b)
, the simplicial complex
\mathbb{S}_{1}
is depicted for
n=3
and
n=4
respectively. In these figures, the simplex in blue corresponds to
\Delta_{n}
and
J_{0}
, the simplex in orange represents
M_{1}
and
J_{1}
,
the simplices in purple are
M_{i}
and
J_{i}
for
2\leq i\leq n
.
The simplicial complex
\mathbb{S}_{1}
around the origin
\mathbf{0}
describes how the components of
\mathcal{J}_{1}
intersect at the origin. Here the origin
\mathbf{0}
is the vertex obtained from the gluing of
v_{0},w_{1,0},\ldots,w_{n,0}
. In particular, the singularity type is described by the complex
\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{1}
.

(a)

Simplicial complex
\mathbb{S}_{1}
for
n=3
.

(b)

Simplicial complex
\mathbb{S}_{1}
for
n=4
.

Figure 13
.
Simplicial complex
\mathbb{S}_{1}
for
n=3,4
.

For
k\geq 2
, the situation is a bit more complicated. The primary decomposition of
\mathcal{J}_{k}
is given in
B.8
, and it has two types of ideals:
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
for
i\in[n]
and
\mathcal{J}_{S}
for
S\subsetneq[k]
with
1\leq|S|\leq\min\{k,n-2\}
. By
4.3
,
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
corresponds to the smoothable component and
\mathcal{J}_{S}
corresponds to the component
\mathop{\mathcal{Hilb}}\nolimits^{m,|S|+1}(C)
. First, we construct a simplicial complex that describes how the ideals
\mathcal{J}_{S}
intersect.
We can associate to the ring
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{k}
the affine space
\mathbb{A}^{nk}
and the real vector space
\mathbb{R}^{nk}
. We denote the standard vectors of
\mathbb{R}^{nk}
by
\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{k},\mathbf{a}_{i,j}
for
i\in[n]
,
j\in[k]
with
i\neq j
, where
\mathbf{b}_{j}
and
\mathbf{a}_{i,j}
are the standard vectors associated to the variables
\beta_{j}
and
\alpha_{i,j}
respectively. Then, we have that

\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{J}_{S})=\mathrm{Span}(\mathbf{b}_{j}:j\in[k]\setminus S,\mathbf{a}_{i,j}:i\in[n]\setminus S,j\in S).

as a real linear subspace.
In
\mathbb{R}^{nk}
, we consider the simplices
\Delta_{nk-1}:=\mathrm{Conv}(\mathbf{b}_{j}:j\in[k],\mathbf{a}_{i,j}:i\in[n]\,j\in[k],\,i\neq j)

and

\widehat{\Delta}_{nk}:=\mathrm{Conv}(\mathbf{0},\Delta_{nk-1})
.
Moreover, we associated to
\mathcal{J}_{S}
the simplices

(43)

\begin{array}[]{l}\Delta_{S}:=\mathrm{Conv}(\mathbf{b}_{j}:j\in[k]\setminus S,\mathbf{a}_{i,j}:i\in[n]\setminus S,j\in S)=\mathbb{V}(\mathcal{J}_{S})\cap\Delta_{nk-1},\\
\widehat{\Delta}_{S}:=\mathrm{Conv}(\mathbf{0},\Delta_{S})=\mathbb{V}(\mathcal{J}_{S})\cap\widehat{\Delta}_{nk}.\end{array}

Note that
\widehat{\Delta}_{S}
is the cone of
\Delta_{S}
. Geometrically,
\Delta_{S}
is the polytope associated to the projective space defined by
\mathcal{J}_{S}
in
\mathbb{P}^{nk-1}
and
\widehat{\Delta}_{S}
is the polytope associated to the cone over this projective space, i.e. the polytope associated to
\mathbb{V}(\mathcal{J}_{S})
in
\mathbb{A}^{nk}
.

Lemma 5.4
.

The simplices
\Delta_{S}
for
S\subsetneq[k]
with
1\leq|S|\leq\min\{k,n-2\}
form a simplicial complex denoted by
\mathbb{S}_{k}^{\prime}
. Similarly, the simplices
\widehat{\Delta}_{S}
for
S\subsetneq[k]
with
1\leq|S|\leq\min\{k,n-2\}
form a simplicial complex denoted by
\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{k}^{\prime}
.
Moreover, we have that

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\Delta_{S}\cap\Delta_{S^{\prime}}=\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{J}_{S}+\mathcal{J}_{S^{\prime}})\cap\Delta_{nk-1}&\text{ and }&\widehat{\Delta}_{S}\cap\widehat{\Delta}_{S^{\prime}}=\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{J}_{S}+\mathcal{J}_{S^{\prime}})\cap\widehat{\Delta}_{nk}\end{array}

for
S,S^{\prime}\subsetneq[k]
with
1\leq|S|,|S^{\prime}|\leq\min\{k,n-2\}
.

Proof.

Let
S,S^{\prime}\subsetneq[k]
with
1\leq|S|,|S^{\prime}|\leq\min\{k,n-2\}
. Then, the linear subspace
\mathbb{V}(\mathcal{J}_{S}+\mathcal{J}_{S^{\prime}})
is generated by the vectors
e_{i}
for
i\in[k]\setminus(S\cup S^{\prime})
and
f_{i,j}
for
i\in[n]\setminus(S\cup S^{\prime})
and
j\in S\cap S^{\prime}
. Using (
43
), we get that

\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta_{S}\cap\Delta_{S^{\prime}}=\mathbb{V}(\mathcal{J}_{S})\cap\mathbb{V}(\mathcal{J}_{S^{\prime}})\cap\Delta_{nk-1}=\mathbb{V}(\mathcal{J}_{S}+\mathcal{J}_{S^{\prime}})\cap\Delta_{nk-1}=\\
\mathrm{Conv}(\mathbf{b}_{i}:i\in[k]\setminus(S\cup S^{\prime}),\mathbf{a}_{i,j}:i\in[n]\setminus(S\cup S^{\prime})\text{ and }j\in S\cap S^{\prime}).\end{array}

In particular,
\Delta_{S}\cap\Delta_{S^{\prime}}
is a face of both
\Delta_{S}
and
\Delta_{S^{\prime}}
, and hence they form a simplicial complex. For
\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{k}^{\prime}
, the proof follows from the fact that it is the simplicial complex obtained by taking the cone of
\mathbb{S}_{k}^{\prime}
over the origin.
∎

From
5.4
, we conclude that the simplicial complex
\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{k}^{\prime}
around
\mathbf{0}
describes how the linear spaces
\mathbb{V}(\mathcal{J}_{S})
intersect. Since
\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{k}^{\prime}
is the cone of
\mathbb{S}_{k}^{\prime}
over the origin, such combinatorics are also encoded in
\mathbb{S}_{k}^{\prime}
.

.

n=3
,
m=3
and

\mathcal{J}_{S}

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathcal{J}_{\{1\}}=\langle\beta_{1},\alpha_{1,2},\alpha_{3,2}\rangle&\text{and}&\mathcal{J}_{\{2\}}=\langle\beta_{2},\alpha_{2,1},\alpha_{3,1}\rangle.\end{array}

Then,
\mathbb{V}(\mathcal{J}_{\{1\}})\cap\mathbb{V}(\mathcal{J}_{\{2\}})
is the origin
\mathbf{0}
. Similarly, the simplices associated to them are

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\widehat{\Delta}_{\{1\}}=\mathrm{Conv}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{b}_{2},\mathbf{a}_{2,1},\mathbf{a}_{3,1})&\text{and}&\widehat{\Delta}_{\{2\}}=\mathrm{Conv}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{b}_{1},\mathbf{a}_{2,1},\mathbf{a}_{3,2}).\end{array}

In this case,

Example 5.6
.

Fix
n=3
,
m=4
and
k=3
, and focus on the singular point
[\langle x_{1}^{2},x_{2}^{2},x_{3}^{2}\rangle]
. Such a point corresponds to the middle vertex of
\mathcal{K}_{3}^{[3]}
in
Fig.

5
. The corresponding ring is
\mathcal{S}_{k}=\mathbb{C}[\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3},\alpha_{1,2},\alpha_{1,3},\alpha_{2,1},\alpha_{2,3},\alpha_{3,1},\alpha_{3,2}]
, and we have three possible ideals of the type
\mathcal{J}_{S}
:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\mathcal{J}_{\{1\}}=\langle\beta_{1},\alpha_{1,2},\alpha_{1,3},\alpha_{2,3},\alpha_{3,2}\rangle,&\mathcal{J}_{\{2\}}=\langle\beta_{2},\alpha_{2,1},\alpha_{2,3},\alpha_{1,3},\alpha_{3,1}\rangle&\text{and}&\mathcal{J}_{\{3\}}=\langle\beta_{3},\alpha_{3,1},\alpha_{3,2},\alpha_{1,2},\alpha_{2,1}\rangle.\end{array}

Then
\mathbb{V}(\mathcal{J}_{\{a\}})\cap\mathbb{V}(\mathcal{J}_{\{b\}})
is
\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\beta_{c}])
for
\{a,b,c\}=[3]
. Similarly, the simplices associate to them are

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\Delta_{\{1\}}=\mathrm{Conv}(\mathbf{b}_{2},\mathbf{b}_{3},\mathbf{a}_{2,1},\mathbf{a}_{3,1}),&\Delta_{\{2\}}=\mathrm{Conv}(\mathbf{b}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{3},\mathbf{a}_{1,2},\mathbf{a}_{3,2})&\text{and}&\Delta_{\{3\}}=\mathrm{Conv}(\mathbf{b}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{2},\mathbf{a}_{1,3},\mathbf{a}_{2,3}).\end{array}

In this case,
the simplicial complex
\mathbb{S}_{2}
is depicted in
Fig.

14(b)
. The simplicial complex
\widehat{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}_{3}
is the cone over
\mathbb{S}_{2}^{\prime}
.

Next, we add to our simplicial complexes the polytopes associated to the ideals of the form
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
in
B.3
. For
i\not\in[k]
, the ring
\mathcal{S}_{k}^{\prime}/\mathcal{Q}_{i}
is the ring

\mathbb{C}[\alpha_{i,1},\ldots,\alpha_{i,k},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}]/\langle\alpha_{i,r}\beta_{r}-\alpha_{i,s}\beta_{s}:1\leq r<s\leq k\rangle.

The ideal of this quotient corresponds to the ideal (
65
). Since the ideal is homogeneous and toric, we can associate to
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
the polytopes
P_{i,k}
, corresponding to the projective toric variety, and

\widehat{P}_{i,k}
, which is the cone over
P_{i,k}
and corresponds to the corresponding affine toric variety.
By
B.6
,
\widehat{P}_{i,k}
is the polytope
P_{k}
and
\widehat{P}_{i,k}
is the cone over
P_{k}
. We refer to (
66
) for the definition of
P_{k}
.
We label the vertices of
P_{i,k}
and
\widehat{P}_{i,k}
slightly different than the label done for
P_{k}
. The vertex
\mathbf{0}
of
P_{k}
is denoted by
a_{i,1}
in
P_{k,i}
. Similarly, the vertices
\mathbf{e}_{1}
,
\mathbf{e}_{2}
and
\mathbf{e}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2}
are labeled by
b_{2}
,
a_{i,2}
and
b_{1}
respectively. The vertices
\mathbf{e}_{j}
and
\mathbf{e}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2}-\mathbf{e}_{j}
are labeled by
a_{i,j}
and
b_{j}
for
3\leq j\leq k
. The extra point we get after taking the cone over
P_{k}
is denoted by
\mathbf{0}
.

\mathbb{V}(I_{k-1})\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}

). Note that
I_{a}

) for

P_{1}

I_{1}

.

. Then,

P_{k-1}
and
\mathbf{e}_{k}
.
Then,
\widehat{P}_{k,k}
is the cone of
P_{k,k}
.

,

,

, and

respectively. Similarly, the vertices
\mathbf{e}_{j}
and
\mathbf{e}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2}-\mathbf{e}_{j}
for

are labeled by
a_{k,j}
and

Now, we build the polyhedral complexes
\mathbb{S}_{k}
and
\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{k}
by adding to the complex
\mathbb{S}_{k}^{\prime}
and
\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{k}^{\prime}
the polytopes
P_{i,k}
and
\widehat{P}_{i,k}
respectively.
By
B.6
, for
i\not\in[k]
, the facets of
P_{i,k}
are simplices whose vertices are

(44)

\{a_{i,j}:j\in S\}\cup\{b_{j}:j\in[k]\setminus S\}

for
S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,k\}
. We denote such a facet by
F_{S}
.
We can associate to the facet
F_{S}
of
P_{i,k}
a face of the simplicial complex
\mathbb{S}_{k}^{\prime}
as follows. We consider the face of
\Delta_{S}
spanned by the vectors

\mathbf{b}_{j}
for
j\in[k]\setminus S
and
\mathbf{a}_{i,j}
for
j\in S
. Such face is isomorphic to
F_{S}
by identifying the vertices
b_{j}
and
a_{i,j}
of
F_{S}
with the vectors
\mathbf{b}_{j}
and
\mathbf{a}_{i,j}
respectively. Similarly, for
i\in[k]
we can identify each each facet of
P_{i,k}
with an isomorphic face of the complex
\mathbb{S}_{k}^{\prime}
by identifying the vertices
a_{i,j}
and
b_{j}
with the vectors
\mathbf{a}_{i,j}
and
\mathbf{b}_{j}
respectively.
The polyhedral complex
\mathbb{S}_{k}
is the complex obtained by adding to the simplicial complex
\mathbb{S}_{k}^{\prime}
the polytopes
P_{i,k}
